M\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}\xrightarrow{(\phi,\mathrm{id})}\phi(M)\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}\hookrightarrow F\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}

yields a section

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{t}}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{M}

over M, and so a line plot
\phi_{t}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{1})
that differentiates to
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}
.
∎

Remark 2.18

(
Tangent vectors, paths, and derivations).

Let
\phi_{t}\in\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)(\mathbbm{R}^{1})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(y(\mathbbm{R}^{1}),\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)\big)

be a
\mathbbm{R}^{1}
-plot
of fields with
\phi_{t=0}=\phi\in\Gamma_{M}(F)
with induced derivation

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}

(28)

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle\longmapsto{\partial_{t}}(f\circ\phi_{t})|_{t=0}\,.

It is obvious that the induced derivation actually depends only on the germ
\mathbbm{R}^{1}
-plots around
0\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}
.
In other words, any two plots
\phi_{t},\phi^{\prime}_{t}:\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times M\rightarrow F
which agree on some open
(-\epsilon,\,+\epsilon)\times M\subset\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times M

define the same derivation. However, unlike the finite-dimensional manifold case, we see no obvious reason why this derivation
depends only on the corresponding tangent vector at
\partial_{t}\phi_{t}|_{t=0}\in T_{\phi}(\mathcal{F})=\Gamma_{M}(\phi^{*}VF)
.
Hence it might not necessarily descend to an action of tangent vectors at
\phi
.
11
11

11

Ref.
[
IZ13
, §6.54]
claims something similar,
but the dependence proved is in fact between germs and tangent vectors of
\mathbbm{R}^{1}
-plots.
However,
this
does hold
for the subset of
local
function(al)s
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\subset C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
,
which correspond to functions that factor through the infinite jet bundle, as we will make precise in the next section
with Definition
3.20
and later on with Remark
6.10
.
This potential ‘pathology’ of tangent vectors and smooth functions
is automatically cured if we work in the better category of infinitesimally thickened smooth sets, where smooth maps preserve
the infinitesimal structure – by definition (see
[
GS24
]
).

As it is, the would-be tangent bundle above is simply a set. Since it is naturally identified with sections of
a bundle, we may apply Def.
2.12
to naturally view it as a smooth set.

Definition 2.19

(
Smooth tangent bundle to field space).

The smooth tangent bundle to a field space
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)
is defined by

\displaystyle T\mathcal{F}:=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(VF)\,\,,

(29)

as the smooth set of sections of
\Gamma_{M}(VF)
via Def.
2.12
.

In particular, an
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plot of the tangent bundle
T\big(\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)\big)
corresponds to a pair
(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k}_{0}},\phi^{k}_{0})

of
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-parametrized sections over
M
such that

\textstyle{VF\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{{\phi}^{k}_{0}}
\textstyle{F\,}

commutes. Note that this immediately shows that the fiber-wise
\mathbbm{R}
-linear structure of the plain set bundle

\Gamma_{M}(VF)\rightarrow\Gamma_{M}(F)
extends to a smooth
y(\mathbbm{R})
-linear map

\displaystyle+:T\mathcal{F}\times T\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}

(30)

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{Z}^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{k}},\,\mathcal{Z}^{2}_{\phi^{k}_{0}}\Big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{Z}^{1}_{\phi_{0}^{k}}+\mathcal{Z}^{2}_{\phi^{k}_{0}}

By arguing as in Lem.
2.17
, any such map
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k}_{0}}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M\rightarrow VF

may be (non-uniquely) represented
12
12

12

An
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plot of the tangent bundle
T\mathcal{F}
is equivalently a section
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\phi^{k}_{0}}

of the pullback bundle
V(\mathsf{pr}_{2}^{*}F)\cong\mathsf{pr}_{2}^{*}(VF)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M
. The proof of Lem.
2.17

then applies verbatim to produce a map
\Psi^{k}_{t}:\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times\mathsf{pr}_{2}^{*}F\rightarrow\mathsf{pr}_{2}^{*}F
which differentiates to
\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{\phi}

along the image of
\phi^{k}_{0}
.
by an
\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plot
\phi^{k}_{t}
of
\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)
such that

\phi^{k}_{t=0}=\phi^{k}_{0}
, and so we might often write

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k}_{0}}=\partial_{t}\phi^{k}_{t}|_{t=0}\,.

(31)

As expected, the evident projection
\Gamma_{M}(VF)\rightarrow\Gamma_{M}(F)
extends to smooth projection map

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{F}}:T\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k}_{0}},\phi^{k}_{0}\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi^{k}_{0}\,.

Hence, we may define smooth vector fields in this infinite-dimensional setting as actual geometrical smooth sections of
the tangent bundle.

Definition 2.20

(
Vector fields on field space).

The set of smooth vector fields on the field space
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)
is defined as smooth sections of
its tangent bundle

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{F}):=\big\{\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}\,|\,\pi_{\mathcal{F}}\circ\mathcal{Z}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}}\big\}\,.

(32)

That is, smooth maps
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}
such that the diagram of smooth sets

\textstyle{T\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{\mathcal{F}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\,}

commutes.

Let us unwind the definition slightly. On
*
-plots, such a section defines a map of sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}:\Gamma_{M}(F)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{M}(VF)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}

which assigns a tangent vector
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\in T_{\phi}\big(\Gamma_{M}(F)\big)=\Gamma_{M}(\phi^{*}VF)
to every field
configuration
\phi\in\Gamma_{M}(F)
, and so a vector field in the intuitive sense. The smoothness condition is
then the further requirement that under this point-wise assignment
13
13

13

This interpretation of smoothness only
holds for concrete smooth sets, i.e., diffeological spaces with an underlying notion of points.
In particular, it does not generalize to non-concrete smooth sets as stated, and hence will also not generalize
when working with smooth fermionic spaces
[
GS24
]
.
,
smooth

\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots of field configurations,
i.e., smoothly
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-parametrized sections
\phi^{k}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M\rightarrow F
, are mapped to
smooth

\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots of tangent vectors, i.e., smoothly
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-parametrized sections
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi^{k}}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M\rightarrow VF
.
The most common examples of smooth vector fields on field spaces arising in physics are
local
vector fields,
that is vector fields that factor through the infinite jet bundle of
J^{\infty}_{M}F
- in a sense that we will make fully
precise in
section

6.1
.

Vector fields on field space
\mathcal{F}
should be interpreted as ‘infinitesimal smooth diffeomorphisms’, in direct analogy with the finite-dimensional case,
as will become clear in the next examples. Firstly, the definition of diffeomorphisms in smooth sets is immediate.

Definition 2.21

(
Diffeomorphisms of the field space).

The set of
diffeomorphisms
of
\mathcal{F}
, i.e., smooth automorphisms of
\mathcal{F}
, is defined as the subset invertible self-morphisms
of smooth sets,

\mathrm{Diff}(\mathcal{F}):=\big\{\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\;|\;\exists\,\mathcal{P}^{-1}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\big\}\,\subset\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\,.

In finite-dimensional smooth manifolds, vector fields can obtained by differentiating smooth 1-parameter families of diffeomorphisms, i.e.,
smooth paths of diffeomorphisms. This analogously carries over to the case of the field space
\mathcal{F}
. A useful intermediate step to deduce this differentiation process is via the smooth set of paths in the field space, constructed as an application of Def.
2.9
.

Definition 2.22

(
Path space of fields).

The
smooth path space
of fields is defined as

P(\mathcal{F}):=\big[y(\mathbbm{R}^{1}),\mathcal{F}\big]\,.

That is,
the smooth set with
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots
14
14

14

In this description, maps of probes
f:\mathbbm{R}^{k^{\prime}}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{k}
act on plots via the pullback
(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{1}}\times f)^{*}:\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)(\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k})\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)(\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k^{\prime}})
.

\big[y(\mathbbm{R}^{1}),\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)\big](\mathbbm{R}^{k}):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(y(\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k})\,,\,\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)\big)\cong\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)(\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times\mathbbm{R}^{k})\,.

For each
t_{0}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
there is a corresponding smooth projection

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}_{t_{0}}:P(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\phi^{k}_{t}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi^{k}_{t=t_{0}}

which evaluates each path (of
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots) of fields at
t_{0}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}
. Furthermore, the projection at
t_{0}=0
obviously
factors through the smooth tangent bundle of
\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)
via

\displaystyle\partial_{t}|_{t=0}:P(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\phi^{k}_{t}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\partial_{t}\phi^{k}_{t}|_{t=0}\,,

which by Lem.
2.17
(and its application to
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots) is an epimorphism (see Eq. (
31
) and its footnote).

Example 2.23

(
Vector fields as infinitesimal diffeomorphisms).

There is a natural notion of a smooth 1-parameter family of diffeomorphisms on
\mathcal{F}
, making use of the canonical smooth mapping
space
[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}]
of Def.
2.9
. This is a
\mathbbm{R}^{1}
-plot of self-morphisms

\mathcal{P}_{t}\in[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}](\mathbbm{R}^{1}):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(y(\mathbbm{R}^{1})\times\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}\big)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathcal{F}\,,\,P(\mathcal{F})\big)

that ‘is a diffeomorphism for each
t_{0}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}
’, where the latter isomorphism is the internal hom property.
15
15

15

Alternatively (and equivalently),
there is a canonical way to supply
\mathrm{Diff}(\mathcal{F})
with a smooth set structure and then consider its line plots instead.
In fact, there exists a sub-object of automorphisms
\mathbold{Aut}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}]
of the self-mapping space
in any sheaf category, whose plots are given by

\mathbold{Aut}(\mathcal{F})(U):=\big\{\tilde{f}=(\mathsf{pr}_{U}\times f):y(U)\times\mathcal{F}\rightarrow y(U)\times\mathcal{F}\,\,|\,\,\exists\,\tilde{f}^{-1}=(\mathsf{pr}_{U}\times g):y(U)\times\mathcal{F}\rightarrow y(U)\times\mathcal{F}\big\}.

Explicitly, it is a smooth map

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{t}:\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{P}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi^{k}_{t}

such that the composition along the projections

\mathrm{ev}_{t_{0}}:P(\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F))\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{t=t_{0}}:\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi^{k}_{t=t_{0}}

is invertible for any
t_{0}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}
. Consider now any such 1-parameter family
\mathcal{P}_{t}\in[\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F}](\mathbbm{R}^{1})
that
starts at the identity map, i.e.,
\mathcal{P}_{t=0}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}}
. Equivalently, this is a section of the bundle

\mathrm{ev}_{t=0}:\mathbf{P}(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
and hence composing along the projection

\partial_{t}|_{t=0}:P(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow T(\mathcal{F})
yields a vector field

\displaystyle\partial_{t}|_{t=0}\circ\mathcal{P}_{t}:\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\partial_{t}(\phi^{k}_{t})|_{t=0}

on
\mathcal{F}
. Hence the ‘infinitesimal part’ of a 1-parameter diffeomorphism connected to the identity is indeed a vector
field on the field space
\mathcal{F}
. On the other hand, unlike the finite-dimensional manifold case, one should not in general expect
to be able to integrate vector fields to 1-parameter families of diffeomorphisms.
16
16

16

In finite dimensions, integral
curves along vector fields always exists - at least for a small ‘time’ interval
(-\epsilon,\epsilon)\subset\mathbbm{R}^{1}
. This is not necessarily true in
the infinite-dimensional smooth set setting.

Note that any smooth 1-parameter family of diffeomorphisms
\mathcal{P}_{t}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbold{P}(\mathcal{F})
connected to the identity defines a derivation

\displaystyle C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})

(33)

where a function
\phi^{k}\mapstochar\rightarrow g\circ\phi^{k}
is mapped to the smooth function
\phi^{k}\mapstochar\rightarrow{\partial_{t}}(g\circ\phi^{k}_{t})|_{t=0}
, extrapolating
Eq. (
26
). As with Remark (
2.18
), this derivation is not necessarily determined
by the induced vector field
\partial_{t}|_{t=0}\circ\mathcal{P}_{t}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{F})
. However, this
does hold
on the subset of local functions
on
\mathcal{F}
, where every
local
vector field defines a derivation of
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
, as will be detailed in Rem.

6.10
. More generally, this potential pathology of vector fields will be automatically cured
when we consider our spaces as thickened smooth sets
[
GS24
]
, where every vector field will necessarily define a derivation.

Example 2.24

(
Diffeomorphisms for vector-valued field space via the target).

In the case of vector-valued field space with
F=M\times W
(Ex.
2.11
), bundle automorphisms correspond
to diffeomorphisms of the target
g:W\rightarrow W
.

17
17

17

This holds for any
\sigma
-model field space, i.e., with target any manifold
N\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
.

For instance, general linear transformations constitute a particular example, whereby choosing a basis
\{e_{a}\}_{a=1,\cdots,N}
for
W\cong\mathbbm{R}^{N}
,

\displaystyle g:W

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow W\,

\displaystyle e_{a}

\displaystyle\longmapsto g^{b}_{\,\,a}\cdot e_{b}

for
\big[g^{b}_{\,\,a}\big]\in\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbbm{R})
. There is an induced diffeomorphism on field space given by postcomposition
of (plots of) fields

\displaystyle g_{*}:\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto g\circ\phi^{k}\,.

It follows that a 1-parameter family of such transformations, i.e.,

\displaystyle g_{t}:\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times W

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow W\,

\displaystyle(t,w^{a}\cdot e_{a})

\displaystyle\longmapsto w^{a}\cdot g^{b}_{\,\,a}(t)\cdot e_{b}

for some
\big[g^{b}_{\,\,a}(t)\big]:\mathbbm{R}^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}(n,\mathbbm{R})
, defines a
1
-parameter family of field space diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{t}=(g_{t})_{*}:\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{P}(\mathcal{F})\,

\displaystyle\phi^{k}=\phi^{k,a}\cdot e_{a}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi^{k}_{t}=\phi^{k,a}\cdot g^{b}_{\,\,a}(t)\cdot e_{b}\,.

Assuming
g_{t=0}=\mathrm{id}_{W}
is the identity map on
W
, with
\big[A^{b}_{\,\,a}\big]=\big[\dot{g}^{b}_{\,\,a}(0)\big]\in\mathfrak{gl}(n,\mathbbm{R})

the induced Lie algebra element, the differentiation of the previous example (Ex.
2.23
)
explicitly yields the smooth vector field
\mathcal{Z}^{A}=\partial_{t}|_{t=0}\circ(g_{t})_{*}
on field space,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{A}:\mathcal{F}\cong[M,W]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T(\mathcal{F})\cong[M,W\times W]\,

\displaystyle\phi^{k}=\phi^{k,a}\cdot e_{a}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big(\phi^{k}\,,\,\phi^{k,a}\cdot A^{b}_{\,\,a}\cdot e_{b}\big)\,.

This is a rigorous way to make sense of the smooth vector field which in the physics literature is often denoted
abusively
18
18

18

Via an (a priori) unjustified analogy to the coordinate formulas of smooth vector fields in the
finite-dimensional setting.
by

\mathcal{Z}^{A}(\phi)=\phi^{a}\cdot A^{b}_{\,\,a}\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{b}}\,,

and more often as an ‘infinitesimal transformation of the field’

\delta_{A}\phi^{b}=A^{b}_{\,\,a}\cdot\phi^{a}\,.

Completely analogously, any translation on
W
acting as
w\mapstochar\rightarrow w+c
for some
c=c^{a}\cdot e_{a}\in W
, defines a diffeomorphism
on field space by postcomposition. The induced vector field of such (1-parameter families of) translations is often denoted as

\mathcal{Z}^{c}(\phi)=c^{a}\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{a}}\,\hskip 42.67912pt\mathrm{or}\hskip 42.67912pt\delta_{c}\phi^{a}=c^{a}\,,

and is in particular ‘constant’ in field space.

Example 2.25

(
Diffeomorphisms for vector-valued field space via the base).

Consider the case of a trivial field bundle
F=M\times W
and let
f:M\rightarrow M
diffeomorphism of the base spacetime.
There is an induced diffeomorphism on field space acting by precomposition of (plots of) fields

\displaystyle f^{*}:[M,W]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow[M,W]

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi^{k}\circ\big(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}},f\big)\,.

It follows that a smooth
1
-parameter family of spacetime diffeomorphisms

f_{t}:\mathbbm{R}^{1}\times M\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow M

induces a
1
-parameter family of field space diffeomorphisms

\displaystyle(f_{t})^{*}:\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{P}(\mathcal{F})\,

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\phi^{k}_{t}=\phi^{k}\circ\big(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}},f_{t}\big)\,.

Assuming
f_{t=0}=\mathrm{id}_{M}
is the identity map on
M
, with induced vector field

v=\partial_{t}f_{t}|_{t=0}=v^{\mu}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\in\Gamma(TM)
on spacetime, the differentiation
of Ex.
2.23
immediately yields the vector field
\mathcal{Z}^{v}=\partial_{t}|_{t=0}\circ(f_{t})^{*}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}^{v}:[M,W]

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow[M,W\times W]\,

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big(\phi^{k}\,,\,\mathrm{d}_{M}\phi^{k}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}},v)\big)\,,

where
\mathrm{d}_{M}\phi^{k}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times TM\rightarrow TW\cong W\times W
is the de Rham differential on
M
. In local coordinates for
M

and a basis for
W
,
\mathrm{d}_{M}\phi^{k}=\partial_{\mu}\phi^{k,a}\cdot\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\cdot e^{a}
, and so

\mathrm{d}_{M}\phi\circ v=\mathbb{L}_{\nu}(\phi^{a})\cdot e_{a}=\partial_{\mu}\phi^{a}\cdot v^{\mu}\cdot e_{a}
, where
\mathbb{L}_{\nu}
is the Lie derivative
along
\nu\in\Gamma(TM)
. In the physics literature, such vector fields are denoted by

\mathcal{Z}^{v}(\phi)=\mathbb{L}_{v}(\phi^{a})\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{a}}=\partial_{\mu}\phi^{a}\cdot v^{\mu}\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{a}}\,,

or even by

\delta_{\nu}\phi^{a}=\mathbb{L}_{\nu}(\phi)^{a}=\nu^{\mu}\cdot\partial_{\mu}\phi^{a}\,,

and one says “the field
\phi
transforms as a scalar”. More than often in physics, the spacetime
M
is supplied with a metric
g
,
and the vector field
v\in\Gamma(TM)
a Killing vector field. A particular instance is Minkowski space
(M,g)=(\mathbbm{R}^{4},\eta)
,
with Killing vector fields
v\in\mathfrak{iso}(1,3)
being elements of the Poincaré Lie algebra.

Remark 2.26

(
Diffeomorphisms via field bundle automorphisms).

Generally, any field bundle automorphism
\tilde{f}:F\rightarrow F

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M}

covering a diffeomorphism
f:M\rightarrow M
(not necessarily the identity), yields a field space diffeomorphism

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\tilde{f}\circ\phi^{k}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}},f^{-1})\,.

One may consider 1-parameter families of such, and differentiate to obtain smooth vector fields on
\mathcal{F}
. In this picture,
Ex.
2.24
corresponds to the case of the trivial bundle
F=M\times W
with
\tilde{f}=(\mathrm{id}_{M},g)

covering the identity on
M
, while Ex.
2.25
corresponds to

\textstyle{M\times W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f,\,\mathrm{id}_{W})}
\textstyle{M\times W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{M\,.}

We note, however, that for a general field fiber bundle
F\rightarrow M
and a given diffeomorphism
f:M\rightarrow M
, the pullback
f^{*}\phi
of
a field is a section of
f^{*}F\rightarrow M
and
not
of
F\rightarrow M
. That is, to act on the actual space of fields via
\mathrm{Diff}(M)
,
it is indeed necessary to lift the given spacetime diffeomorphism to a bundle morphism
\tilde{f}:F\rightarrow F
. However, this is not always possible,
contrary to the case of the trivial field bundle. Bundles with such a lifting property are called ‘‘gauge natural” bundles
19
19

19

An archetypical example
is the tangent/cotangent bundles and tensor powers thereof. The prime example of a field space employing natural bundles is pure gravity in its

(0,2)
-tensor metric formulation, whereby the field space consists of (symmetric, non-degenerate) sections of
\wedge^{2}T^{*}M
, i.e.,

\mathrm{(}Met)_{M}:=\Gamma^{\mathrm{sym.nondeg.}}_{M}(\wedge^{2}T^{*}M)
. Any diffeomorphism
f:M\rightarrow M
lifts to a bundle morphism which allows
to pull back metrics, defining a smooth diffeomorphism
\mathcal{P}:=f^{*}:\mathrm{(}Met)_{M}\rightarrow\mathrm{(}Met)_{M}
. Strictly speaking, this is only valid
for pure gravity, or at most coupled to fields that are sections of trivial or natural bundles. In particular, it breaks down when (fermionic)
spinors enter the picture.
(see
[
KMS93
]
).
Nevertheless, for many purposes of field theory
(e.g., for computing conserved currents, see
section

6.1
) it suffices to lift only the infinitesimal version
of spacetime diffeomorphisms, i.e., vector fields on
M
. That is, there exists a short exact sequence

\displaystyle 0\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{F}(VF)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{F}(TF)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{F}(\pi^{*}TM)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow 0\,,

a splitting of which can be used to lift infinitesimal diffeomorphisms on spacetime, i.e., elements of
\Gamma_{M}(TM)
.
20
20

20

Splitting
as vector spaces is sufficient.
Indeed, such a lift given by a connection on
F\rightarrow M
is a Lie algebra homomorphism, if and only if the connection is flat.
Such a splitting is a
connection

on
F\rightarrow M
, which always exists. In the relevant field bundles of physics, the corresponding connection is often fixed as a background structure in the choice of
the Lagrangian density (Def.
3.9
), or is otherwise a dynamical field itself. The data in this situation is enough to define an induced vector
field on
\mathcal{F}
, from that on the spacetime
M
(see
[
CGRZ20
, §5.3]

for a particular instance in the case of the first-order formulation of general relativity, and sources therein).
We will not expand on this intricacy further in this manuscript.

Having defined a notion of a smooth tangent bundle
T\mathcal{F}
on a field space
\mathcal{F}
, which does have a natural fiber-wise linear structure (Eq. (
30
)), there is a natural corresponding notion of differential forms, as fiber-wise linear and antisymmetric maps out of
T\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 2.27

(
Differential forms on field space).

The set of differential m-forms on
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)
is defined as

\displaystyle\Omega^{m}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{fib.lin.an.}}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(T^{\times m}\mathcal{F}\,,\,\mathbbm{R}\big)\,,

(34)

i.e., smooth real-valued, fiber-wise linear antisymmetric maps with respect to the fiber-wise linear structure (Eq. (
30
)) on the
m
-fold fiber product

T^{\times m}\mathcal{F}:=T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{F}}T\mathcal{F}

of the tangent bundle over the
\mathcal{F}
.

Exactly as with finite-dimensional manifolds, the collection of differential forms of all degrees forms a graded
\mathbbm{R}
-vector space

\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}):=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbbm{N}}\Omega^{m}(\mathcal{F})\,,

with a well-defined notion of a wedge product
\wedge
and contraction
\iota_{X}
for any vector field
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{F})
. What is not at all obvious is the existence of a differential along
\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{F}}:\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}(\mathcal{F})
(see Rem.
7.2
). Nevertheless, we will see in
section

7
that this is precisely the correct notion of differential forms to host the subspace of smooth and
local
forms on the field
space, whereby such a differential exists and satisfies a Cartan calculus with respect to
local
vector fields. This will encode in a rigorous manner
the kind of differential forms and their manipulations exactly as they are performed in the physics literature.

Remark 2.28

(
Notions of tangent bundles).

The construction of a kinematical tangent bundle from Ex.
2.15

and Def.
2.19
can be generalized to any diffeological space
[
Hec96
]
, i.e.,
any smooth set with underlying set of ‘enough’ points.
However, in general, there are different choices for the smooth set structure on the resulting tangent bundle set

[
CSW14
]
[
CWu16
]
.
Even worse, one may also define tangent vectors extrinsically as derivations of the corresponding smooth function algebra
[
CSW14
]
[
CWu16
]
, since as we
alluded to the two notions are not necessarily equivalent. As this text will make clear, the correct and useful notion of
the tangent bundle to a field space is that of Def.
2.19
. In fact, will show how we may define the tangent bundle
to the field space, and in fact any (thickened) smooth set,
as a particular mapping space smooth set in a canonical manner via synthetic differential geometry in
[
GS24
]
,
bypassing these ambiguities and recovering the above discussion.

2.3
Classifying space of de Rham forms

All the previous examples of smooth sets have a notion of underlying
sets of points
, in that

\mathcal{G}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\subset\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{Set}}(\mathbbm{R}^{k},G_{s})
for some plain set
G_{s}\in\mathrm{Set}
, for each probe space

\mathbbm{R}^{k}\in\mathrm{CartSp}
(cf. Def.
2.9
). We say such smooth sets are

concrete
. The full subcategory of concrete smooth sets is naturally identified with
diffeological spaces

[
IZ13
]
. However, not all smooth sets are concrete and in fact
there are useful non-concrete generalized smooth spaces.
21
21

21

This is, in fact, generically the case for fermionic field spaces
which constitute
super
smooth sets
[
GS24
]
.
One such smooth set is the “moduli space of de Rham n-forms”, which in particular
allows for an alternative definition of forms on an arbitrary smooth set.

Definition 2.29

(
n
-forms as smooth set).

For each
n\in\mathbbm{N}
, we define the
moduli space of de Rham
n
-forms

\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}\in\mathrm{SmthSet}
by

\displaystyle\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}):=\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\,.

(35)

That is, the assignment of
n
-forms on each
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\in\mathrm{CartSp}
.

This is a presheaf, since forms pull back along maps of manifolds, and further a sheaf since locally defined forms glue.
Naturally, the equivalent sheaf
\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}
over the category of all manifolds is given by the same formula.
Note that for
n=0
this sheaf is the same as the embedding of
\mathbbm{R}
into smooth sets, since

\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=\Omega^{0}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=y(\mathbbm{R})(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\,.

In particular,
\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{0}
is a concrete smooth set. For
n>0
,
\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}
is no longer
concrete, and in fact quite unintuitive from the point-set perspective. Indeed, for a fixed
n\in\mathbbm{N}
,

\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=\{*\}

for all
k<n
and so there is a single point in
\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}
, with all
p
-dimensional ‘plots’
degenerating to the same point for
k<n
. However, for
k\geq n

\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\,,

is an infinite set, and so there exists an infinite number of
k
-dimensional plots in

\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}
for
k\geq n
.

We now consider operations on the moduli spaces. For any
n\geq 0
, there exists a smooth map incarnation of the de Rham differential

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}:\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n+1}

(36)

defined plot-wise by

\displaystyle\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{n+1}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})

\displaystyle\omega_{\,\mathbbm{R}^{k}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\mathrm{d}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\omega_{\,\mathbbm{R}^{k}}

for each
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\in\mathrm{CartSp}
and
\omega_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\in\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
, with
\mathrm{d}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}

being the usual
de Rham differential on
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
. This constitutes a smooth map, since the usual de Rham differential
commutes with pullbacks of manifolds. Thus, the collection of the moduli spaces of
n
-forms for
n\geq 0

inherits a cochain complex structure, now in smooth sets
\big(\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet},\mathrm{d}\big)\in\mathrm{Ch}(\mathrm{SmthSet})
,
with the module structure being that of the smooth set real numbers
y(\mathbbm{R})\in\mathrm{SmthSet}
.
Similarly, for any
n,m\geq 0
there exists a smooth map incarnation of the wedge product

\displaystyle\wedge:\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}\times\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{m}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n+m}

(37)

defined plot-wise by

\displaystyle\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\times\Omega^{m}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{n+m}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})

\displaystyle\big(\omega_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}},\omega^{\prime}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\omega_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\wedge_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\omega^{\prime}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}

for each
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\in\mathrm{CartSp}
, with
\omega_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\in\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
and

\omega^{\prime}_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\in\Omega^{m}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
, respectively. It follows that
\mathrm{d}
is a (graded) differential with respect to
\wedge
,
due to the corresponding plot-wise property. The above natural
maps of smooth sets can be summarized in the following lemma.

Lemma 2.30

(
Induced structure on the moduli space).

The differential graded commutative
\mathbbm{R}
-algebra (DGCA) structure of forms
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
on each
Cartesian space
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\in\mathrm{Cart}
induces a DGCA structure on the moduli space of forms

\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet}\in\mathrm{SmthSet}
. That is, the triple

\big(\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}},\wedge\big)
forms a DGCA in
smooth sets,

\displaystyle\big(\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet},\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}},\wedge\big)\in\mathrm{DGCA}(\mathrm{SmthSet})\,,

(38)

as a module over
y(\mathbbm{R})\in\mathrm{SmthSet}
.

The name “moduli space” is justified, for the moment only partially, for the following reason.
For any smooth manifold
M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
viewed as a smooth set, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}(y(M),\,\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n})\cong\Omega^{n}(M)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthMfd}}^{\mathrm{fib.lin.}}(T^{\times n}M,\,\mathbbm{R})

(39)

where the first isomorphism follows by Yoneda Lemma
2.4
and the equivalence
\mathrm{Sh(Cart)}\cong\mathrm{Sh(Man)}
.
That is, smooth maps from a manifold
M
to the smooth set
\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}
coincide with the set of

n
-forms on
M
, hence
\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}
indeed serves as a space that ‘modulates
n
-forms on manifolds’.
We may extend the above identification to a definition of
n
-forms which applies to any smooth set

\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{SmthSet}
.

The following is essentially the definition of forms in the restricted case of Diffeological spaces
[
IZ13
]
,
now extended to non-concrete smooth sets. This definition will generalize to the synthetic differential context of
(infinitesimally) thickened smooth sets (see
[
GS24
]
), where as far as we know its original conception along with its full
classifying nature arose.

Definition 2.31

(
De Rham forms on smooth sets).

The set of
smooth de Rham
n
-forms
on a generalized smooth space

\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{SmthSet}
is defined by

\displaystyle\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathcal{F},\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n})\,.

(40)

That is, since morphisms of smooth sets are given
by natural transformations,
an
n
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{n}(\mathcal{F})
is an (functorial) assignment

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})

\displaystyle\phi_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\omega_{f_{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}}\,,

mapping each ‘
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plot’ in
\mathcal{F}
to an n-form on
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
. The de Rham differential and wedge product of forms on a smooth space are defined
naturally by composing with their universal incarnations (
36
) and (
37
).

Definition 2.32

(
Differentials and wedge products of forms).

(i)
The de Rham 1-form
\mathrm{d}S\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})
of a smooth map
S:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow y(\mathbbm{R})

is defined as the composition

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}S:\mathcal{F}\xlongrightarrow{S}y(\mathbbm{R})\cong\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{0}\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}}\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{1}\,.

(41)

(ii)
Similarly, the differential
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}\omega\in\Omega^{n+1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})
of an
n
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{n}(\mathcal{F})

is defined as the composition

\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}\omega:\mathcal{F}\xlongrightarrow{\;\omega\;}\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}}\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n+1}\,.

(iii)
Finally, the wedge product of two forms
\omega\in\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})
,
\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{m}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})

is defined as the composition

\displaystyle\omega\wedge\omega^{\prime}:\mathcal{F}\xlongrightarrow{\;(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\;}\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}\times\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{m}\xlongrightarrow{\;\wedge\;}\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n+m}\,.

By Lem.
2.30
, it follows that the collection of differential forms on
any smooth space
\mathcal{F}
inherits the structure of a DGCA over the real numbers

(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F}),\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}},\wedge)\,\in\mathrm{DGCA}_{\mathbbm{R}}\,.

However, this notion of forms on a smooth set, say a field space
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(VF)
, does not have an obvious ‘contraction’
operation with the corresponding vector fields. In particular, this is in contrast to the differential forms of Def.
2.27
.

Remark 2.33

(
Classifying nature of
\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\bullet}
).

Although we have defined smooth differential forms as maps into a ‘classifying/moduli space’, having all the standard algebraic
properties one would expect, it is not clear what exactly they ‘classify/modulate’.

(i)
For the case of representable objects, i.e.,
finite-dimensional manifolds, they do classify the familiar differential forms as fiber-wise linear maps out of the
tangent bundle (
39
).
However, as we have noted before,
there are several notions of ‘a tangent bundle’ for a general smooth set, each of which having its own drawbacks.

(ii)
We will come back to the fully-fledged classifying nature of this sheaf once we enrich our site with infinitesimal spaces.
In that setting, there will be a natural notion of a tangent bundle for any generalized smooth space;
see
[
GS24
]
.
For those generalized spaces that have a fiber-wise linear structure on their tangent bundle, this sheaf will
classify forms in the traditional sense of fiber-wise linear maps out of the tangent bundle, as in the particular case of
Def.
2.27
for field spaces;
see
[
GS24
]
. In the current restricted setting of smooth sets, one can still show that a certain subset of
local
differential
forms (Def.
7.5
) be viewed equivalently as maps into the classifying space
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{dR}}

(Lem.
7.9
).

(iii)
Nevertheless, we note that the moduli space of
n
-forms does find important applications even in the current restricted setting
of sheaves over Cartesian spaces. For example:

(a)

In
[
IZ13
]
it is shown that forms on quotients of finite-dimensional manifolds

22
22

22

Such quotients do not necessarily result in smooth manifolds (e.g., the irrational torus), but they may be naturally viewed
as smooth sets since arbitrary colimits exist in
\mathrm{SmthSet}
.
in the above sense define a sensible de Rham cohomology.

(b)

For
M^{d}
a smooth spacetime manifold of dimension
d
, the mapping space

\big[M^{d},\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{d+2}\big]
may be seen as a valid home for “anomaly polynomials” of Green-Schwarz type.
Namely, these are
(d+2)
-forms
I_{d+2}(\phi^{k})
associated naturally with
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots of field configurations
\phi^{k}
,
and hence in total form a natural system of differential forms on
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M
,
which is just a single smooth map from
\mathcal{F}
into the mapping space
[M^{d},\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{d+2}]
.
Crucially, such a map is necessarily trivial on fields and
\mathbbm{R}^{1}
-plots of fields, since

[M^{d},\mathbold{\Omega}^{d+2}_{\mathrm{dR}}](\mathbbm{R}^{k})\cong\Omega^{d+2}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M^{d})
– which vanishes for

k\leq 1
(i.e., the mapping space is also not concrete). Nevertheless, there is non-trivial data in the anomaly polynomial,
encoded on higher
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots of fields. If we assume that the integral of such forms over
M^{d}
exists
(for instance, assuming that
M^{d}
is compact) then this construction serves to produce a smooth de Rham 2-form on the field space, as follows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\big[M^{d},\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{d+2}\big]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{2}_{\mathrm{dR}}\;.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overset{\mathclap{\raisebox{1.0pt}{
\scalebox{.7}{\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}
probe
}
}}}{\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\times M^{d}\xrightarrow{\phi^{k}}F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{d+2}(\phi^{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int_{M^{d}}I_{{}_{d+2}}(\phi^{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\scalebox{.7}{\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}
\begin{tabular}[]{c}$\mathbbm{R}^{k}$-parametrized\\
family of fields\end{tabular}
}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\scalebox{.7}{\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}
\begin{tabular}[]{c}anomaly polynomial\\
in
$\Omega^{d+2}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M^{d})$\end{tabular}
}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathclap{\scalebox{.7}{\bf\color[rgb]{0.05,0.25,0.65}
\begin{tabular}[]{c}local anomaly\\
in
$\Omega^{2}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})$\end{tabular}
}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{{}_{d+2}}}

curvature of anomaly line bundle on field space

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\int_{M^{d}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\longmapsto}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\longmapsto}

(42)

As indicated in the diagram, if the
I_{d+2}
are indeed the anomaly polynomials of a Green-Schwarz type anomaly,
then the 2-form on field space exhibited by the composite arrow is a precise incarnation of what is commonly referred to
as the curvature of the “anomaly line bundle” (cf.
[
Fre02
, p. 21]
, where our “
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
” appears as “
T
”).

Of course, in the entire anomaly line bundle
corresponding to (
42
) appears similarly as a map
\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbf{B}\mathrm{U}(1)_{\mathrm{conn}}

to the smooth stack of circle bundles with connection (
202
). Alternatively, for the usual case of
local

anomaly polynomials, these may be equivalently interpreted as
(d,2)
-local forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
(Def.
7.5
),
with the relation of the two interpretations being via Lem.
7.9
.

(c)

Similarly,
invariant
polynomials of degree
n
(such as underlying higher Chern-Simons forms) are naturally interpreted as maps out
of a moduli stack
\mathbf{B}G_{\mathrm{conn}}
(
202
) into the classifying sheaf of differential forms
(see
[
FSS12
, p. 65]
[
FSS13
, p. 2]
[
FH13
]
[
Sc13a
, §5.4.3]
[
FSS14
, §3.4]
):

\mathbf{B}G_{\mathrm{conn}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{2n}\,.

(43)

Smooth structure on de Rham forms of a smooth set.

By construction, the set of smooth de Rham forms on a smooth space
\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})
may be promoted to
a smooth set using the internal hom (
2.9
)

{\mathbold{\Omega}}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}(\mathcal{F})(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(y(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\times\mathcal{F},\,\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}\big)\,.

This does contain what one would interpret as ‘smoothly
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-parametrized n-forms on
\mathcal{F}
’, but
is much larger. To see this, consider the case when
\mathcal{F}=y(M)
is a manifold where, by the Yoneda Lemma,

\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}\big(y(M)\big)(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(y(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\times y(M),\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}\big)\cong\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M)\,.

Instead, we would like to consider
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-parametrized forms on a manifold
M
as the subalgebra of vertical

n
-forms along
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M\rightarrow M
, i.e.,

\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{Vert}}\big(y(M)\big)(\mathbbm{R}^{k})=\Omega^{n}(\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times M)\big/\Omega^{n\geq 1}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\cong C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\hat{\otimes}\Omega^{n}(M)\cong{\mathbold{\Gamma}}_{M}(\wedge^{n}T^{*}M)(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\,,

where
\hat{\otimes}
denotes the completed projective tensor product of
\mathbbm{R}
-vector spaces.
Analogously working internally to smooth sets, we instead promote
\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})
to a smooth set vertically by

\displaystyle\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR,Vert}}(\mathcal{F})(\mathbbm{R}^{k}):=C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\hat{\otimes}\Omega^{n}(\mathcal{F})=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathcal{F},\mathbold{\Omega}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{n}\hat{\otimes}C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\big)\,,

(44)

where the tensor product on the right-hand side is meant to be computed object-wise.

23
23

23

It can be checked that this smooth set structure corresponds exactly to the so-called “concretification”
of the mapping space
[\mathcal{F},\mathbold{\Omega}^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}]
(see
[
Sc13a
, §1.2.3.3]
).

In this case, the de Rham differential also extends vertically to a smooth map

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{Vert}}:\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR,Vert}}(\mathcal{F})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{n+1}_{\mathrm{dR,Vert}}(\mathcal{F})

(45)

as
C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\hat{\otimes}\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})\xlongrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{d}}C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\hat{\otimes}\Omega^{n+1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})
,
for each
\mathbbm{R}^{k}\in\mathrm{Cart}
. Similarly, the wedge product may be extended vertically to a smooth map

\wedge_{\mathrm{Vert}}:\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR,Vert}}(\mathcal{F})\times\Omega^{m}_{\mathrm{dR,Vert}}(\mathcal{F})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{n+m}_{\mathrm{dR,Vert}}(\mathcal{F}).

Remark 2.34

(
Cotangent bundles of smooth sets).

For the case of a manifold
M\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
,
1
-forms are naturally identified with sections of the cotangent bundle

\Omega^{1}(M)\cong\Gamma_{M}(T^{*}M)
, and analogously for
n
-forms. The vertical smooth structure on
n
-forms defined
above coincides with that on sections of a vector bundle, as in Def.
2.12
. However, for
a general smooth space
\mathcal{F}
, there is no natural notion of a cotangent bundle over it – but we still want to
think of de Rham forms on
\mathcal{F}
as sections of a would-be bundle,
hence the vertical smooth structure.
24
24

24

For the case when
\mathcal{F}
is a diffeological space, there is a (somewhat indirect)
way to construct a bundle whose sections coincide with
\Omega^{n}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F})
; see
[
IZ13
, Ch.6]
.

To recap, there is a consistent and very general definition of smooth forms on any generalized
smooth space, finite or infinite-dimensional, concrete or not, which evades the use of any
functional analysis. In
section

5.3
, we will describe how this notion of forms on the field space
may be used to encode the traditional variation of an action functional (albeit not in the most straightforward manner,
see Rem.
5.41
). To that end, we now move into the description of the infinite jet bundle,
as it forms a crucial component of (local) variational field theory.

3
Local Lagrangians

We first recall the traditional infinite-dimensional Fréchet, locally pro-manifold structure
on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
, following the description of
[
Ta79
]
[
Sau89
]
[
KS17
]
. Then, viewing the infinite
jet bundle as a smooth set, we describe how to employ smooth maps out of it to define (smooth)
local
Lagrangians,
local

currents and charges, and
local
functionals on the corresponding smooth field space
\mathcal{F}
.
We conclude by defining the appropriate notion of a (finite and smooth) symmetry of a local Lagrangian field theory. In the subsequent sections,
we will use the smooth set description of the infinite jet bundle to naturally construct its tangent bundle, vector fields, and differential
forms internally within SmoothSet while, in parallel, showing these constructions recover all the corresponding standard
notions employed in the classical literature.

In
[
GS24
]
, we will detail how one may equivalently detect (or define) the jet
bundle directly within (formal) smooth sets, therefore bypassing
the infinite-dimensional technicalities mentioned below, and further setting the scene to define the appropriate notion of jet
bundles in the fermionic setting. In particular, an (infinite) jet (
48
) of a fiber bundle
F\rightarrow M

at a point
p
in the base will be equivalently a section of
F
over the
‘infinitesimal neighborhood’
of
p\in M
.

3.1
Infinite jet bundles as locally pro-manifolds

Let
\pi_{M}:F\rightarrow M
be a fiber bundle and
p\in M

a point in the base. For any
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, the set of
k^{th}
-order jets of sections at
p\in M

is traditionally defined as the equivalence classes of (local) sections such that their partial derivatives agree, on some
(and hence any) local chart, up to order
k
at
p
:

\displaystyle J^{k}_{p}(F):=\Big\{j^{k}_{p}\phi=[\phi]\,\big|\,\phi\sim\phi^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{M}(F)\iff\partial_{I}\phi^{a}(p)=\partial_{I}{\phi^{\prime}}^{a}(p)\hskip 8.5359pt\forall\,\,0\leq|I|\leq k\Big\}\,,

(46)

where
I
denotes (symmetric) multi-indices. The collection of
k
-jets forms a finite-dimensional manifold

J^{k}_{M}(F)=\underset{p\in M}{\bigcup}J^{k}_{p}(F)\quad\in\quad\mathrm{SmthMfd}\;,

and in particular a fiber bundle over
M
for each
k\in\mathbbm{N}
. For instance, a compatible coordinate chart
\{x^{\mu},u^{a}\}

on a trivialization of
F\rightarrow M
induces a natural coordinate chart on
J^{1}_{M}(F)
denoted by

\{x^{\mu},u^{a},u^{a}_{\mu}\}
. Similarly, there are induced coordinate charts

\big\{x^{\mu},\{u_{I}^{a}\}_{|I|\leq k}\big\}:=\big\{x^{\mu},u^{a},u^{a}_{\mu},u^{a}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}},\cdots,u^{a}_{\mu_{1}\cdots\mu_{k}}\big\}

on each
J^{k}_{M}F
, with extra coordinates corresponding
to the higher partial derivatives appearing in the definition of a
k
-jet (and hence symmetric in the base indices).
There is a natural sequence of smooth maps
\pi^{k}_{k-1}:J^{k}_{M}(F)\rightarrow J^{k-1}_{M}(F)
,
in particular surjective submersions,

which ‘forget’ the highest derivatives, and so a diagram of smooth manifolds

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{k-1}_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{1}_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{0}_{M}(F)\cong F\,.

Furthermore, there is a canonical map of smooth sections, the
k^{th}
-order
jet prolongation

\displaystyle j^{k}:\Gamma_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{M}\big(J^{k}_{M}(F)\big)

(47)

where
j^{k}\phi(p):=j^{k}_{p}\phi
, for each
k\in\mathbbm{N}
.

The infinite jet bundle
J^{\infty}_{M}F
is supposed to be the projective limit of the above diagram. It exists when
computed in the category Set of sets (or topological spaces), and so each fiber
J^{\infty}_{p}(F)
will consist of
equivalence classes of sections whose partial derivatives agree to arbitrary order, i.e.,

\displaystyle J^{\infty}_{p}(F)=\Big\{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi=[\phi]\;\big|\;\phi\sim\phi^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{M}(F)\iff\partial_{I}\phi^{a}(p)=\partial_{I}{\phi^{\prime}}^{a}(p)\hskip 8.5359pt\forall\,\,0\leq|I|\Big\}\,.

(48)

Set-theoretically, this guarantees the existence of a projection map

\pi_{k}\equiv\pi^{\infty}_{k}:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)

for each
k\in\mathbbm{N}
and a map of sections, the
infinite jet prolongation

\displaystyle j^{\infty}:\Gamma_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Gamma_{M}\big(J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\big)\,,

(49)

where
j^{\infty}\phi(x):=j^{\infty}_{x}\phi
. However, the limit does not exist in
\mathrm{SmthMfd}
since it is an
infinite limit of manifolds of increasing dimension, hence necessarily infinite-dimensional.

A natural way to evaluate
the limit is via the fully faithful embedding
\mathrm{SmthMfd}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{FrMfd}
of finite-dimensional manifolds
into Fréchet manifolds, whereby the limit
J^{\infty}_{M}F:=\mathrm{lim}_{k}^{\mathrm{FrMfd}}J^{k}_{M}F
exists
as an infinite-dimensional, paracompact manifold by virtue of the maps in the diagram being surjective submersions

[
Ta79
]
[
Sau89
]
[
KS17
]
.

Definition 3.1

(
Fréchet jet bundles).

The
infinite jet bundle

J^{\infty}_{M}F
is the (para-compact and Hausdorff) Fréchet manifold defined by the limit

J^{\infty}_{M}(F):=\mathrm{lim}^{\mathrm{FrMfd}}_{k}\,\,J^{k}_{M}(F)\quad\in\quad\mathrm{FrMfd}\,

whose local model is

\mathbbm{R}^{\infty}=\mathrm{lim}^{\mathrm{FrMfd}}_{k}\,\,\mathbbm{R}^{k}\,\,\in\,\,\mathrm{FrMfd}
, with

\big\{x^{\mu},\{u_{I}^{a}\}_{0\leq|I|}\big\}:=\big\{x^{\mu},u^{a},u^{a}_{\mu_{1}},u^{a}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}},\cdots\big\}

being the local coordinate charts.

From our point of view, the
manifold and explicit chart description of the infinite jet bundle will mostly play an auxiliary role, so as to make
contact with the existing literature. The universal properties will play a more central role. For instance, the projections

\pi^{\infty}_{k}:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)
are identified with the universal cone projections, and hence become
smooth Fréchet maps. More generally, such infinite-dimensional limits of finite-dimensional manifolds form a subcategory
of Fréchet manifolds
[
KS17
]
.

Definition 3.2

(
Locally pro-manifolds).

We define the category of
locally pro-manifolds

\mathrm{LocProMan}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{FrMfd}\,,

to be the full subcategory of Fréchet manifolds consisting of projective limits of finite-dimensional manifolds.

Note that, by Prop.
2.7
, locally pro-manifolds also embed fully faithfully into smooth sets via

\displaystyle\mathrm{LocProMan}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{FrMfd}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pty\hskip 8.19447pt}}\mathrm{SmthSet}\,.

The defining properties of such infinite-dimensional manifolds, and in particular of the resulting infinite-dimensional manifold

\displaystyle J^{\infty}_{M}(F):=\mathrm{lim}_{k}^{\mathrm{FrMfd}}\,\,J^{k}_{M}(F)\quad\in\quad\mathrm{LocProMan}

(50)

with which we are concerned is the characterization of smooth Fréchet maps,

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{FrMfd}}\big(J^{\infty}_{M}(F),\Sigma\big)

and
\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{FrMfd}}\big(\Sigma,J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\big)
, with codomain and domain being a finite-dimensional manifold
\Sigma\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
,
respectively.
The characterization of smooth maps into
J_{M}^{\infty}(F)
is straightforward.

Lemma 3.3

(
Smooth maps into the jet bundle).

Let
\Sigma\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
be a finite-dimensional manifold. A map of sets
f:\Sigma\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}(F)
is smooth if
and only if

\pi^{\infty}_{k}\circ f:\Sigma\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)

is smooth for each
k\in\mathbbm{N}
. Furthermore, smooth maps
f:\Sigma\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}(F)
are in
1-1 correspondence with families of smooth maps
\{f_{k}:\Sigma\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)\,|\,k\in\mathbbm{N}\}
such that

\textstyle{\Sigma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{k_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{k_{2}}}
\textstyle{J^{k_{2}}_{M}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{k_{2}}_{k_{1}}}
\textstyle{J^{k_{1}}_{M}(F)}

commutes for each pair
k_{2}\geq k_{1}
.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the universal cone property of the limit

\displaystyle J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{k-1}_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\cdots\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{1}_{M}(F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{0}_{M}(F)\cong F\,.

∎

In particular, this result holds for all
\Sigma\in\mathrm{CartSp}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SmthMfd}
, and so gives
a more explicit description of the smooth set

y(J^{\infty}_{M}F):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{FrMfd}}(-,J^{\infty}_{M}F)|_{\mathrm{CartSp}}\quad\in\quad\mathrm{SmthSet}

incarnation of the infinite jet bundle along the embedding of Prop.
2.7
.
Note that limits in SmoothSet are computed objectwise, which means that the smooth set above is equivalently the limit formed in smooth sets

\displaystyle y(J^{\infty}_{M}F)\cong\mathrm{lim}_{k}^{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\,y(J^{k}_{M}F)\,.

(51)

by first embedding each finite-dimensional jet bundle. Concretely, we may consider
\mathbbm{R}^{n}
-plots of
y(J^{\infty}_{M}F)
as families
of compatible plots of each
J^{k}_{M}F
,

y(J^{\infty}_{M}F)(\mathbbm{R}^{n})\cong\Big\{\big\{s^{n}_{k}:\mathbbm{R}^{n}\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}F\;\;\big|\;\;\pi^{k}_{k-1}\circ s^{n}_{k}=s^{n}_{k-1}\big\}_{k\in\mathbbm{N}}\Big\}\,.

In particular, an infinity jet
j^{\infty}_{p}\phi\in J^{\infty}_{M}F
, i.e., a
*
-plot in
y(J^{\infty}_{M}F)
is equivalently represented by the
compatible family of
k
-jets
\{j^{k}_{p}\phi=\pi_{k}(j^{\infty}_{p})\}_{k\in\mathbbm{N}}
, as expected by the underlying set-theoretic limit (
48
).

Remark 3.4

(
Locally pro-manifolds as smooth set limits).

The same statements hold for any locally pro-manifold

G^{\infty}=\mathrm{lim}_{k}^{\mathrm{FrMfd}}G^{k}\in\mathrm{LocProMan}
.
That is,

y(G^{\infty}):=y\big(\mathrm{lim}_{k}^{\mathrm{FrMfd}}G^{k}\big)\cong\mathrm{lim}_{k}^{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(y(G^{k})\big)\,,

i.e., the embedding
y:\mathrm{FrMfd}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SmthSet}
reflects projective limits of finite-dimensional manifolds.
In fact,
y:\mathrm{FrMfd}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{SmthSet}
reflects all limits of finite-dimensional manifolds, and hence preserves
all limits in
\mathrm{LocProMan}
, by definition of the latter.

Moreover, the limit property implies that the infinite jet prolongation (
49
) maps smooth sections of
F
to smooth sections of

J^{\infty}_{M}(F)
, since composition with each of
\pi^{\infty}_{k}
gives a smooth section of
J^{k}_{M}(F)
. Smooth sections of the infinite jet bundle
naturally form a smooth set
\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(J^{\infty}F)
as in Def.
2.12
, i.e., by considering smoothly

\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-parametrized sections of
J^{\infty}_{M}(F)
. Moreover, the infinite jet prolongation canonically extends (uniquely) to a
smooth
map

\displaystyle j^{\infty}:\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(J^{\infty}F)

(52)

\displaystyle\phi^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto j^{\infty}\phi^{k}

vertically. Explicitly, for
\phi^{k}\in\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
a smoothly
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-parametrized section (Def.
2.12
),

j^{\infty}\phi^{k}\in\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(J^{\infty}F)(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
is defined by
j^{\infty}\phi^{k}(x,u):=j^{\infty}\big(\iota^{*}_{u\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{k}}\phi^{k}\big)(x)
,
i.e., the prolongation is applied point-wise with respect to the probe space. It is immediate to see that the assignment is functorial
with respect to pullbacks of maps
f:\mathbbm{R}^{m}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{k}
of Cartesian probe spaces, hence defining a map of smooth sets.

The characterization of smooth maps out of
J^{\infty}_{M}(F)
is more delicate and, for that purpose, we use the following
[
Ta79
]
[
KS17
]
.

Proposition 3.5

(
Smooth maps out of the jet bundle).

Let
\pi_{k}:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)
be the canonical projection for each
k\in\mathbbm{N}
.
A function
f:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
(a map of sets) is smooth if and only if locally around every point
x\in J^{\infty}_{M}(F)

it factors through
\pi_{k}:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)
for some
k\in\mathbbm{N}
. That is, for each
x\in J^{\infty}_{M}(F)

there exists a
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, a neighborhood
U\subset J^{k}_{M}(F)
around
\pi_{k}(x)\in J^{k}_{M}(F)
and a smooth function of (finite-dimensional)
manifolds
\tilde{f}^{k}_{U}:J^{k}_{M}(F)|_{U}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{J^{\infty}_{M}(F)|_{\pi_{k}^{-1}(U)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k}}
\textstyle{J^{k}_{M}(F)|_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}^{\,k}_{U}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}\;.}

We note that the proof of this proposition, and later the discussion of Rem.
4.12
,
are the only place where analytical details regarding Fréchet theory contribute
to our discussion.
The rest of the presentation is mainly based on universal properties of smooth sets along with the above result,
where necessary. The result is readily extended if the target manifold
\mathbbm{R}
is replaced by
\mathbbm{R}^{n}
,
and consequently by any finite-dimensional manifold
N\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
.

Corollary 3.6

(
Smooth maps valued in manifolds).

Let
\Sigma\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
be a finite-dimensional manifold. A function
f:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow\Sigma
(of sets)
is smooth if and only if, locally around every point
x\in J^{\infty}_{M}(F)
, it factors through

\pi_{k}:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)
for
some
k\in\mathbbm{N}
.

Proof.

Consider first the case where
\Sigma=\mathbbm{R}^{2}
, with
f=J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{2}
a map of sets. It is a smooth map
of Fréchet manifolds if and only if
f_{i}:=\mathsf{pr}_{i}\circ f:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
is smooth for both
i=1,2
.
By Prop.
3.5
, this is the case if and only if around each
x\in J^{\infty}_{M}(F)
there
exist
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbbm{N}
, neighborhoods
U_{1}\subset J^{k_{1}}_{M}(F),\,U_{2}\subset J^{k_{2}}_{M}(F)
and smooth functions

\tilde{f}^{\,k_{i}}_{U_{i}}:J^{k_{i}}_{M}(F)|{U_{i}}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
such that the diagrams

\textstyle{J^{\infty}_{M}(F)|_{\pi_{k_{i}}^{-1}(U_{i})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k_{i}}}
\textstyle{J^{k_{i}}_{M}(F)|_{U_{i}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}^{\,k_{i}}_{U_{i}}}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}}

commute for
i=1,2
. Assume (without loss of generality) that
k_{2}\geq k_{1}
and let
\pi^{k_{2}}_{k_{1}}:J^{k_{2}}_{M}(F)\rightarrow J^{k_{1}}_{M}(F)

denote the canonical projection.
Define
\bar{f}^{\,k_{1}}_{U}:=(\pi^{k_{2}}_{k_{1}})^{*}\tilde{f}^{\,k_{1}}_{U_{1}}:J^{k_{2}}_{M}(F)|_{U}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
,
where
U=U_{2}\cap\pi^{-1}(U_{1})
, then the diagram

\textstyle{J^{\infty}_{M}(F)|_{\pi_{k_{2}}^{-1}(U)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f_{1},f_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k_{2}}}
\textstyle{J^{k_{2}}_{M}(F)|_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\big(\bar{f}^{\;k_{1}}_{U},\;\tilde{f}^{\;k_{2}}_{U_{2}}\big)}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{2}}

commutes since each of its compositions with the two projections
\mathsf{pr}_{i}:\mathbbm{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
commute.
Hence, a map
f:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{2}
is smooth if and only if it locally factors through finite order jet bundles.

The case for the codomain being
\mathbbm{R}^{n}
follows similarly by induction of the above argument.
Lastly, let
\Sigma
be an n-dimensional manifold, then a map
f:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow\Sigma
is smooth if and only if

\psi\circ f:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)|_{f^{-1}V}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{n}
is smooth for every local chart

\psi:V\subset\Sigma\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbbm{R}^{n}
. By the previous argument, this is true if and only if for each

x\in J^{\infty}_{M}(F)
there exists some
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, a neighborhood
U\subset J^{k}_{M}(F)
of
\pi^{k}(x)
and a
smooth map
\tilde{f}_{U}^{k}:J^{k}_{M}(F)\rightarrow V\subset\Sigma
such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{J^{\infty}_{M}(F)|_{\pi_{k}^{-1}(U)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{k}}
\textstyle{J^{k}_{M}(F)|_{U}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{f}^{\,k}_{U}}
\textstyle{V\subset\Sigma\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{n}}

For each local chart
\psi
, the corresponding diagram commutes if and only if its left triangle commutes,
giving the result.
∎

We may combine the above two results to describe the set of smooth maps

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{LocProMan}}(J^{\infty}_{M}F,G^{\infty})
from the infinite jet bundle to any other
projective limit of finite-dimensional manifolds
G^{\infty}=\mathrm{lim}_{j}^{\mathrm{FrMfd}}G^{j}
.

Proposition 3.7

(
Smooth maps of locally pro-manifolds).

Let
G^{\infty}=\underset{{\rm FrMan}}{{\rm lim}}G^{j}
be any locally pro-manifold.

(i)
A map of sets

f:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow G^{\infty}
is smooth if and only if

p^{\infty}_{j}\circ f:J^{\infty}_{M}F\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow G^{\infty}\xlongrightarrow{\;\;p^{\infty}_{j}\;\;}G^{j}

is smooth for each
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, and hence if each
p^{\infty}_{j}\circ f
locally around every
x\in J^{\infty}_{M}F

locally factors through
\pi_{k}:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)
for some
k\in\mathbbm{N}
, where

p^{\infty}_{j}:G^{\infty}\rightarrow G^{j}
denotes the universal cone projections of
G^{\infty}
.

(ii)
Furthermore,
smooth maps
f:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow G^{\infty}
are in 1-1 correspondence with compatible families of
smooth maps

\{f_{j}:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow G^{j}\,|\,j\in\mathbbm{N}\}
such that

\textstyle{J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{j_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{f_{j_{2}}}
\textstyle{G^{j_{2}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{j_{2}}_{j_{1}}}
\textstyle{G^{j_{1}}}

commutes for each pair
k_{2}\geq k_{1}
, and hence, such that furthermore each
f_{j}
locally factors through

\pi_{k}:J^{\infty}_{M}(F)\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}(F)
for some
k\in\mathbbm{N}
.

Proof.

By the Fréchet limit property of
G^{\infty}
, a map of sets
f:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow G^{\infty}
is smooth if and only if each

p^{\infty}_{j}\circ f:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow G^{j}
, and furthermore each such compatible family of smooth maps

\{f_{j}:J^{\infty}_{M}\rightarrow G^{j}\,|\,j\in\mathbbm{N}\}
defines a smooth map
f:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow G^{\infty}
.
Each of the terms
G^{j}
in the limit are by assumption finite-dimensional manifolds
G^{j}\in\mathrm{SmthMfd}
, and hence by Cor.
3.6
each
f_{j}
locally factors through a finite order jet bundle .
∎

Remark 3.8

(
Pro-manifold vs Fréchet jet bundle).

(i)
The limit over jet bundles may alternatively be computed formally as a pro-object in the categorical sense.
In this case, the resulting object is
not a Fréchet manifold, but simply a
formal

limit of finite-dimensional manifolds instead. A smooth function on such a formal object is
by definition

one that
globally
factors through some
J^{k}_{M}(F)
. Said otherwise, the smooth functions
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{glb}}(J^{\infty}_{M}F)

on the formal limit are the union over
k\in\mathbbm{N}
of those on
J^{k}_{M}(F)
.
25
25

25

Namely, the colimit of the algebras
of functions on each finite order jet bundle. This is, in particular, precisely the definition that
[
An89
]
uses.

Note that these naturally sit inside the algebra of locally finite order functions

C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{glb}}(J^{\infty}_{M}F)\hookrightarrow C^{\infty}(J^{\infty}_{M}F)
, as a sub-algebra.

(ii)
Crucially, however,
globally finite order functions on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
do not form a (petit) sheaf on the underlying topological space, in contrast to those of
locally finite order, and hence the study of the two is of different flavor.
The formal approach is implicit in

[
An84
]
[
An89
]
[
DF99
]
, while
[
Ta79
]
[
Sau89
]
[
KS17
]
employ the Fréchet limit described above.

(iii)
Our choice of the latter is based upon the simple structure of maps into the Fréchet manifold
J^{\infty}_{M}F
of Lem.
3.3
,
and the natural embedding into smooth sets of Prop.
2.7
-- thus viewing it as a proper geometrical space on the same footing with field
spaces. Further support for this choice is given by the observation that the resulting smooth set is equivalently the limit over jet bundles computed directly
in smooth sets,
26
26

26

This does not render the Fréchet picture redundant, for it is this incarnation that provides the intuitive point set
description of smooth maps out of
J^{\infty}_{M}F
.
as in (
51
).

(iv)
We note that the texts
[
Blo23
]
[
De18
]
attempt to view (bosonic) Lagrangian field theory as taking place in the
full subcategory
\mathrm{DflSp}
of smooth sets, while also requiring that the infinite jet bundle is only a formal pro-object.
The drawbacks in that approach are twofold:

(a)

restricting to concrete sheaves, while sufficient for most aspects of bosonic field theory, does not naturally generalize to
include fermionic fields, or infinitesimal structure;

(b)

insisting on viewing the infinite jet bundle as a pro-object requires the introduction of considerably extra categorical machinery,
while still not embedding into
\mathrm{SmthSet}
(or
\mathrm{DflSp}
), and hence not treating all objects appearing on the same categorical and
geometrical footing. The resolution therein is to develop the rest of the field-theoretic concepts in Pro
\mathrm{SmthSet}
(or Pro
\mathrm{DflSp}
).
This is a somewhat heavy conceptual step, and seems unnecessary from our perspective.

3.2
Local Lagrangians, currents and symmetries

In the case of interest to field theory, the above formulation of the infinite jet bundle correctly encodes the explicit
form of local Lagrangian densities.

Definition 3.9

(
Local Lagrangian density).

A local Lagrangian density is a map of smooth sections
\mathcal{L}:\Gamma_{M}(F)\rightarrow\Omega^{d}(M)
of the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}:\Gamma_{M}(F)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{d}(M)

(53)

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\longmapsto L\circ j^{\infty}\phi

where
j^{\infty}
is the jet prolongation
j^{\infty}:\Gamma_{M}(F)\rightarrow\Gamma_{M}(J^{\infty}F)
and
L
is
a
smooth bundle
map

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 14.6014pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-14.6014pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{J^{\infty}_{M}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
