Nomenclature of local and global symmetries).

The adverbs “local” and “global” have several, but related meanings
in the physics literature. In this manuscript, we use local as in the mathematics literature, to mean factoring through an infinite jet bundle,
or to indicate that a certain quantity is defined only on a subspace of an ambient space. In the physics literature, gauge symmetries
are often referred to simply as ‘local symmetries’ in the sense that they are freely parametrized by (gauge) parameters that are not ‘constant’
through space-time. In other words, their value may be chosen to vary ‘locally from point to point in space-time. In contrast, the term
‘global symmetry’ (also called rigid) is reserved for symmetries that are not part of a gauge symmetry, meaning they can be only parametrized by a finite
(or countable) dimensional parameter vector space. More formally, the set of
global
or
rigid
infinitesimal symmetries of
a Lagrangian is defined as the quotient of all local infinitesimal symmetries by the (ideal) of gauge symmetries
(
Rem.
6.22
(iv)
)

\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathcal{L},\mathrm{rig}}(\mathcal{F}):=\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})\,/\,\mathcal{X}^{\mathcal{L},\mathrm{gauge}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\,.

6.3
Cauchy surfaces and obstructions by gauge symmetries

The all-important feature of gauge symmetries, both physically and mathematically, is that they
‘obstruct the unique evolution’

of fields obeying the Euler–Lagrange equations. Let us make this mathematically precise in our current context of smooth sets.

Definition 6.28

(
Sections on infinitesimal neighborhood of submanifold).

Let
\iota_{\Sigma^{p}}:\Sigma^{p}\hookrightarrow M
be a submanifold of spacetime. The
sections on the infinitesimal neighborhood of

\Sigma^{p}
of the bundle
F\rightarrow M
is defined as the set of sections of the infinite jet bundle
J^{\infty}_{M}F
over
\Sigma^{p}
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Sigma^{p}}\big(J^{\infty}_{M}F|_{\Sigma^{p}}\big)\cong\big\{\psi:\Sigma^{p}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F\;\big|\;\pi_{M}\circ\psi=\iota_{\Sigma^{p}}\big\}\,.

(165)

The corresponding smooth set
\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\Sigma^{p}}\big(J^{\infty}_{M}F|_{\Sigma^{p}}\big)
follows as in Def.
2.12
.

The nomenclature will become apparent in
[
GS24
]
, whereby the ‘infinitesimal neighborhood of
\Sigma^{p}
in
M
’ will be a
bona-fide

(thickened) smooth subspace of
M
, over which sections of
F
(and not
J^{\infty}_{M}F
) correspond to the above set. For now, it is enough to note
the interpretation of the definition: Any section
\psi:\Sigma^{p}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F|_{\Sigma^{p}}
(smoothly) determines a particular instance
of the potential values for sections
\phi
of
F\rightarrow M
and their derivatives to any order on the submanifold
\Sigma^{p}
,
\psi(x)=[j^{\infty}_{x}\phi]
.
In other words, one may ask whether there exists a field
\phi\in\mathcal{F}(*)=\Gamma_{M}(F)
whose jet restricts to the chosen section
\psi
on
the infinitesimal neighborhood of
\Sigma^{p}
. Along these lines, choosing the submanifold to be of codimension-1 and demanding further compatibility
with the Euler–Lagrange equations, we may define the smooth set of initial conditions on a submanifold
\Sigma^{d-1}
for a given Lagrangian
field theory
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
on the spacetime
M
.

Definition 6.29

(
Initial data).

Let
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
be a local Lagrangian field theory on
M
and
\Sigma^{d-1}\hookrightarrow M
a submanifold of codimension
1
. The (smooth)
set of
initial data of
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
on
\Sigma^{d-1}
is defined by the subset of (plots of) sections over the infinitesimal
neighborhood of
\Sigma^{d-1}
that factor through the prolongated shell
S_{L}^{\infty}\hookrightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
(Def.
5.12
) of the Lagrangian L,

\displaystyle\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\Sigma^{d-1}):=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\Sigma^{d-1},\mathsf{pr}EL=0}\big(J_{M}^{\infty}F|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big)\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big(J_{M}^{\infty}F|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big)\,.

(166)

In particular, a point
\psi
of
\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\Sigma^{d-1})
is a section of the form

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 12.28284pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 30.28288pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{S^{\infty}_{L}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.46347pt\raise-9.40674pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 104.25517pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 74.8593pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 104.25517pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{J^{\infty}_{M}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 86.25513pt\raise-9.6691pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-12.28284pt\raise-12.1616pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Sigma^{d-1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.10359pt\raise 0.03029pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\psi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.28288pt\raise-3.15433pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 69.46347pt\raise-12.1616pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 37.87315pt\raise-12.1616pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 69.46347pt\raise-12.1616pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 115.85657pt\raise-12.1616pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\,,

and similarly for higher plots.

There is a natural restriction map from the space of fields to sections over the infinitesimal neighborhood of
\Sigma^{d-1}
,
given by the compostion

(-)|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(J^{\infty}_{M}F)\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big(J_{M}^{\infty}F|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big)
.
By definition of the space of initial data on
\Sigma^{d-1}
, this descends to a map

\displaystyle(-)|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\Sigma^{d-1})

(167)

since any on-shell field
\phi:M\rightarrow F
field factors through the prolongated shell via the jet prolongation, and hence
its restriction to any submanifold does as well.

Definition 6.30

(
Cauchy surface).

A (connected) codimension-1 submanifold
\Sigma^{d-1}\hookrightarrow M
is a
Cauchy surface
of the Lagrangian field theory
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
if
the smooth map

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cau}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}:=(-)|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\xlongrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt\sim\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\Sigma^{d-1})

(168)

is a diffeomorphism.

The interpretation is exactly as in the physics literature. At the level of
*
-plots, this entails that there is a bijection between fields
\phi:M\rightarrow F

that satisfy the Euler–Lagrange equations
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}(\phi)=0
on
M
, and the set of initial conditions
\psi:\Sigma^{d-1}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
consistent with
the Euler–Lagrange equations,
\mathrm{Im}(\psi)\subset S_{L}\hookrightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
. In simple words, the derivatives of the on-shell fields along
a Cauchy surface serve as a parametrization of the full space of on-shell fields.

78
78

78

In general, the definition might be relaxed to yield a
diffeomorphism for on-shell fields restricted to an open neighborhood
U
of
\Sigma^{d-1}
. Everything we say here applies to such a modification,
and hence we will not be explicit about it.

In our current setting of smooth sets, we see that this parametrization, if it exists, is in fact
automatically smooth.

Remark 6.31

(
On the order of initial data).

Strictly speaking, in practice, for an Euler–Lagrange differential operator of order
k
,
EL^{k}:J^{k}_{M}F\rightarrow\wedge^{d}T^{*}M\otimes V^{*}F
,
it is sufficient to consider (compatible) initial data in terms of jets along
a hypersurface, up to order
k
. That is, the above definitions are trivially modified by replacing
S^{\infty}_{L}\hookrightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F

with the
k
-order shell
S_{L,k}\hookrightarrow J^{k}_{M}F
(see Rem.
5.17
). In special cases, these smooth sets can be
identified -- but not in general. Nevertheless, everything we say below holds verbatim for both notions of initial data.
79
79

79

For globally finite order
differential operators, it is not hard to see that there is a bijection - and hence a diffeomorphism - between the
images
of the restrictions

\mathrm{Cau}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}:=(-)|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big(J_{M}^{\infty}F|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big)

and
\mathrm{Cau}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}^{k}:=(-)|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\circ j^{k}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big(J_{M}^{k}F|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big)
.
Hence, when
\Sigma^{d-1}
is a Cauchy surface, the two notions of initial data coincide.

Our choice is purely formal, so as to avoid talking about a fixed finite order of the Lagrangian and the
induced Euler–Lagrange operator altogether.

Example 6.32

(
Particle mechanics Cauchy surface).

As a check, we consider the particularly simple example of free (non-relativistic) particle mechanics. Although everything we say generalizes
straightforwardly for a free particle moving in an arbitrary Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
, for the sake of exposition we write formulas explicitly
only for the case of flat, Euclidean space
(\mathbbm{R}^{d},\delta)
. This is, in fact, a special
case of the O(
n
)-model from Ex.
3.10
via the following choices.
Choosing the spacetime to be only ‘time’
M=\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
with its canonical metric, then the Euclidean target
W\cong(\mathbbm{R}^{d},\delta)

is interpreted as the ‘space’, and the field space is the smooth path space
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})=[\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t},\mathbbm{R}^{d}]
.
In the case where the coupling constants vanish, the Lagrangian reduces to the free particle Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\langle\mathrm{d}_{t}\gamma,\star\mathrm{d}_{t}\gamma\rangle=\langle\partial_{t}\gamma,\partial_{t}\gamma\rangle\cdot\mathrm{d}t=\partial_{t}\gamma^{a}\cdot\partial_{t}\gamma_{a}\cdot\mathrm{d}t\,.

The corresponding Euler–Lagrange operator is simply Newton’s Law

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}_{t}\star\mathrm{d}_{t}\gamma=\partial_{t}^{2}\gamma\cdot\mathrm{d}t\,,

and the on-shell space of solutions consists of (plots of) straight lines in
\mathbbm{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}=\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow[\mathbbm{R}_{t}^{1},\mathbbm{R}^{d}]\,,

whose *-plots are simply straight lines
\gamma=v\cdot t+c:\mathbbm{R}_{t}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{d}
. Similarly,
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots are parametrized straight lines

\gamma^{k}=v^{k}\cdot t+c^{k}:\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{R}_{t}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, for arbitrary functions
v^{k}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
and
c^{k}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})
.

The space of positions and velocities at any time instant
t_{0}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
should be a copy of the tangent bundle
T\mathbbm{R}^{d}
,
and as is well known (and obvious) the position and velocity at a time instant fully determine the set of straight lines.
Thus, it is naturally expected that the full space of on-shell fields
\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
is
diffeomorphic

to the tangent
T\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, in an appropriate sense. To see how this description arises from our definition, notice that
the vanishing of the Euler–Lagrange source form
EL=u_{tt}^{a}\cdot\mathrm{d}_{V}u_{a}\cdot\mathrm{d}t\in\Omega^{d=1,1}\big(J^{\infty}_{\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)
, and of its prolongation
\mathsf{pr}EL
,
imposes vanishing conditions on all jets of order
k\geq 2
, and no conditions for
k\leq 1
. Hence its prolongated shell
S^{\infty}_{L}
is
canonically identified with the first jet bundle under the canonical embedding

\displaystyle J^{1}_{\mathbbm{R}_{t}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\big(\gamma(t_{0}),\dot{\gamma}(t_{0})\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big(\gamma(t_{0}),\dot{\gamma}(t_{0}),0,\dots 0\big)\,,

which makes sense globally in this case of trivial bundles. Now, connected submanifolds of codimension-1 of the base
M=\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}

are necessarily single points, i.e., instants of time
t_{0}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
. Hence the smooth set of initial data

\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\{0\})
(Def.
6.29
) along
\{t_{0}=0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
,
has
*
-plots
\psi
that factor through the shell

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 10.50002pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 28.50006pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 28.50006pt\raise-6.64268pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 108.05775pt\raise-12.81496pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.27888pt\raise-12.81496pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 108.05775pt\raise-12.81496pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 171.68385pt\raise-12.81496pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\,,

and similarly for higher plots. Note that such sections are simply points (and plots) of the first jet manifold

J^{1}_{\mathbbm{R}_{t}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbbm{R}^{d})
, and so the smooth set of initial data is simply the Yoneda embedding of the first jet
space
\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\{0\})\cong y(J^{1}_{\mathbbm{R}_{t}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbbm{R}^{d}))
. But the manifold of first jets of
curves is by definition the tangent bundle
J^{1}_{\mathbbm{R}_{t}}(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbbm{R}^{d})\cong_{\mathrm{SmthMfd}}T\mathbbm{R}^{d}
,
and so it follows that

\displaystyle\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\{0\})\cong_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}y(T\mathbbm{R}^{d})\,.

In other words, the smooth set of initial conditions is simply (the Yoneda embedding of) the tangent bundle
T\mathbbm{R}^{d}
of the target
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
,
which shows immediately that the restriction

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cau}_{\{0\}}:=(-)|_{\{t=0\}}\circ j^{1}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle\;\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\;\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\{0\})\cong y(T\mathbbm{R}^{d})

(169)

\displaystyle\gamma^{k}=v^{k}\cdot t+c^{k}

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;(c^{k},v^{k})\,,

is a diffeomorphism. Here the choice of time instant
\{t_{0}\}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
is immaterial, and hence
any time instant is a Cauchy surface – but through a different Cauchy isomorphism. We will revisit this setting in Ex.
7.43
below.

Generally, neither the surjectivity nor the injectivity of the underlying point-set map (
167
) to the initial data
is guaranteed, both of which depend highly on the detailed form of the given
Euler–Lagrange differential operator
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}
. In non-technical terms, surjectivity says that for any choice of initial data

\psi:\Sigma^{d-1}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
, there exists
some
field configuration
\phi:M\rightarrow F
whose jet prolongation restricts
to
\psi
on
\Sigma^{d-1}
, and so
\phi
is thought as the ‘
evolution
’ of the initial condition
\psi
on
M
under the Euler–Lagrange
equations
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}=0
. Injectivity says that in the cases of initial conditions
\psi
for which some evolution
\phi

exists
,
then it is also
unique
.

The general study of such
well-posedness
questions is quite delicate and falls within the realm of analysis of PDEs,
which is outside of the scope of this manuscript. However, an immediate general result that evades analytical arguments is that

finite gauge
symmetries
obstruct
the existence of Cauchy surfaces – by ruling out the injectivity of the underlying point-set map.

Proposition 6.33

(
Gauge symmetry obstructs Cauchy surface).

Let
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}:\Gamma_{M}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
be an infinitesimal (local) gauge symmetry of a Lagrangian field theory
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})

and
\Sigma^{d-1}\hookrightarrow M
a codimension-1 submanifold. Let
e\in\Gamma_{M}(E)
be a (nonzero) gauge parameter with support contained strictly
within
\mathrm{int}(M-\Sigma^{d-1})
. Then, assuming
the infinitesimal symmetry
\mathcal{R}_{e}
is
(a)
integrable and
(b)
not zero on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
(hence not trivial), the restriction to initial data

(-)|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\Sigma^{d-1})

is not a diffeomorphism. In other words, the submanifold
\Sigma^{d-1}
is not a Cauchy surface.

Proof.

Denote by
\mathcal{D}_{(e)}:=\mathcal{D}_{(e),t=1}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}
the diffeomorphism which is the integrated version of the local
\mathcal{R}_{e}

vector field, i.e.,
\partial_{t}\mathcal{D}_{(e),t}|_{t=0}=\mathcal{R}_{e}
. This exists by assumption and hence is in particular
a
local
symmetry (Rem.
6.13
).
By Prop.
5.28
, it preserves the on-shell space of fields

\mathcal{D}_{(e)}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
,
and so if
\phi\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}(*)
is an on-shell field, then so is
\mathcal{D}_{(e)}(\phi)
.
Furthermore, the infinitesimal symmetry
\mathcal{R}_{e}

is by assumption not zero on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
(hence nontrivial, Ex.
6.12
, Ex.
6.13
).
Consequently,
\mathcal{D}_{(e)}
is
not
the identity map
\mathcal{D}_{(e)}\neq\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}}
on the space of on-shell fields, so that
(without loss of generality)

\mathcal{D}_{(e)}(\phi)\neq\phi\,.

Next, since the support of
e
is strictly within
\mathrm{int}(M-\Sigma^{d-1})
, it follows that for any
p\in\Sigma^{d-1}

there exists some open neighborhood

U_{p}\subset M
on which
e
vanishes,
e|_{U_{p}}=0
. It follows that, by the
\mathbbm{R}
-linear dependence on
e
and the petit sheaf
nature of the field space
\mathcal{F}

(Rem.
2.14
), the
local
vector field
\mathcal{R}_{e}
vanishes on
U_{p}
, i.e.,

\mathcal{R}_{e}|_{U_{p}}=0\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}|_{U_{p}})
.
Thus, the corresponding integrated diffeomorphism must be the
identity
when restricted to
U_{p}\subset M
,

\mathcal{D}_{(e)}|_{U_{p}}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}|_{U_{p}}}\,:\,\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}|_{U_{p}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}|_{U_{p}}\,,

for some open neighborhood around
p\in\Sigma^{d-1}
, for
every

p\in\Sigma^{d-1}
.

Finally, to prove the claim of the proposition, it is enough to prove the map is not bijective on
*
-plots. Let
\phi\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}(*)

be any on-shell field configuration. Since local diffeomorphism on
\mathcal{F}
are in particular maps of (petit) sheaves on
M
, we have

\mathcal{D}_{(e)}(\phi)|_{U_{p}}=\mathcal{D}_{(e)}|_{U_{p}}(\phi|_{U_{p}})=\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{F}|_{U_{p}}}(\phi|_{U_{p}})=\phi|_{U_{p}}\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}|_{U_{p}}\,

by which it follows that the infinite jets coincide

j^{\infty}_{p}\big(\mathcal{D}_{(e)}(\phi)\big)=j^{\infty}_{p}\phi\,\in J^{\infty}_{M}F\,,

for every
p\in\Sigma^{d-1}
. That is, both on-shell fields
\mathcal{D}_{(e)}(\phi)
and
\phi
define the
same
initial data on
\Sigma^{d-1}

j^{\infty}\big(\mathcal{D}_{(e)}\phi)|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}=j^{\infty}\phi|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\,:\,\Sigma^{d-1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow S_{L}\hookrightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F\,.

Therefore, there are two
different
on-shell configurations
\mathcal{D}_{(e)}(\phi),\,\phi
with the same initial data
j^{\infty}\phi|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}
, hence the map

(-)|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}(*)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(\Sigma^{d-1})(*)

is
not
injective.
∎

Corollary 6.34

(
Nonexistence of Cauchy surfaces).

Let
\mathcal{R}_{(-)}:\Gamma_{M}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
be an infinitesimal (local) gauge symmetry of a Lagrangian field theory
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})

such that
\mathcal{R}_{e}
is
(a)
integrable and
(b)
not zero on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
, for all gauge parameters
e\in\Gamma_{M}(F)
of compact support.
Then there does not exist any Cauchy surface for
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
.

Proof.

Let
\Sigma^{d-1}
be a codimension-
1
submanifold and choose (nonzero) a gauge parameter
e\in\Gamma_{M}(E)
with compact support in

\mathrm{int}(M-\Sigma^{d-1})
. Such sections exist since
E\rightarrow M
is a locally trivial bundle, by employing bump functions.
By the assumptions on
\mathcal{R}_{e}
, the result of Prop.
6.33
applies. The argument applies
for any candidate submanifold
\Sigma^{d-1}
, and so the result follows.
∎

Example 6.35

(
Instances of nonexistence).

The assumptions of Cor
6.34
are satisfied in all examples of fundamental field theories with gauge symmetries.
We indicate the corresponding ingredients without spelling out the details in two such cases:

(i)
For the theory of General Relativity, the infinitesimal gauge symmetries are parametrized by vector fields
\mathcal{X}(M)=\Gamma_{M}(TM)
on spacetime.
Those of compact support
\mathcal{X}_{c}(M)\subset\mathcal{X}(M)

(
and their induced vector fields on
\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{Met}(M)
)
arise by differentiating diffeomorphisms

\mathrm{Diff}_{c}(M)\subset\mathrm{Diff}(M)

(
and the induced action on
\mathrm{Met}(M)
)
80
80

80

Strictly speaking, the (finite) gauge symmetries
in the case of General Relativity are spacetime covariant symmetries (Def.
3.23
). Nevertheless, the on-shell space of
fields is still preserved (Prop.
5.42
), and hence Prop.
6.33
and
Cor.
6.34
apply with minor modifications.
which are the identity outside a compact submanifold.

(ii)
Similarly for Yang-Mills theory in the trivial topological sector, the infinitesimal gauge symmetries are parametrized by

C^{\infty}(M,\mathfrak{g})=\Gamma_{M}(M\times\mathfrak{g})
.
The gauge parameters of compact support
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,\mathfrak{g})\subset C^{\infty}(M,\mathfrak{g})
arise by differentiating maps
C^{\infty}_{c}(M,G)\subset C^{\infty}(M,G)

which map to the identity
e\in G
outside a compact submanifold (along with the induced vector fields on
\mathcal{F}=\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
by differentiating
the induced finite diffeomorphisms
on
\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
, respectively). A special case of this is (abelian)
G=U(1)
Yang-Mills theory in the guise of electromagnetism from
Ex.
6.21
. By the linearity of the field space
\mathcal{F}=\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{Vert}}(M)
and the constancy of each
vector field
\mathcal{R}_{e}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{F}\times\mathcal{F}
, given by
A^{k}\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathrm{d}_{M}e
, the diffeomorphism corresponding to the
integrated infinitesimal transformation is given on
\mathbbm{R}^{k}
-plots of fields by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{(e)}:\Omega_{\mathrm{Vert}}^{1}(M)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathrm{Vert}}^{1}(M)

\displaystyle A^{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto A^{k}+\mathrm{d}_{M}e\,.

Remark 6.36

(
Gauge equivalence classes).

To preserve the assumption that classical physics is
deterministic
, i.e., with a unique ‘time evolution’, one is forced to consider any two field
configuration
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathcal{F}(*)
of a local field theory
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
which are related by a (finite) gauge transformation as being equivalent.
It follows that a physically viable observable should assign the same value for any gauge equivalent field configuration. One way to implement this
rigorously is to consider the quotient set of fields by gauge transformations, or in our setting, the quotient
smooth

set – a colimit construction which
exists
in smooth sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\,/\,\mathrm{Diff}^{\mathcal{L},\mathrm{gauge}}(\mathcal{F})\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{SmthSet}

(170)

on which all (gauge invariant) local smooth functionals out of
\mathcal{F}
descend to. We note that for this quotient construction to make sense,
it is crucial that the Euler–Lagrange operator is covariant under local (and spacetime covariant) symmetries, which is indeed the case
(
135
).
For the case of electromagnetism, this is the smooth set corresponding to the quotient
\Omega^{1}(M)/\mathrm{d}_{M}\Omega^{0}(M)
.

Remark 6.37

(
On gauge equivalence and redundancy).

We briefly remark on two ways that the viewpoint of gauge symmetries as mere redundancies can be insufficient. The concepts involved in the discussion below
will be rigorously fleshed out in
[
GSS24
]
.

(i)
This treatment is sufficient for most purposes of
classical
field theory with fields being sections of fiber bundles

\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)
. However, for the purposes of ‘gauge fixing’
81
81

81

Roughly, this means finding an equivalent local field theory

(\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
with
no gauge symmetries
. Equivalency, among other conditions, means that the two underlying on-shell spaces should be
diffeomorphic
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}/\mathrm{Diff}^{\mathcal{L},\mathrm{gauge}}(\mathcal{F})\cong\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}}
.
and quantization it is extremely useful to
consider this as a quotient in a homotopical/higher geometrical sense, thus resulting into a
smooth groupoid
instead (see
section

8
).
The
infinitesimal
/
perturbative
(and algebraic) incarnation of this construction appears in the physics literature under
the name
BRST complex
(see
[
HT92
]
).

(ii)
There is another, related, sense in which the phrase ‘gauge equivalence is redundancy’ should be carefully interpreted.
Throughout this text, we have dealt with field spaces being sections of fiber bundles
\mathcal{F}=\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)

over the spacetime
M
,
which are by definition (petit) sheaves on
M
(Rem.
2.14
). This description includes, in particular, globally defined

G
-gauge fields
\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})\cong\Gamma_{M}\big(T^{*}M\times_{M}(M\times\mathfrak{g})\big)
. In other words, principal
G
-bundle connections over
M

in the
trivial topological sector
. Taking into account dynamics via a Lagrangian which is invariant under the action of
C^{\infty}(M,G)
,
one may (or may not) consider the corresponding homotopy quotient groupoid as the actual physical field space. However, gauge fields in the
non-trivial topological sector, i.e.,
G
-connections on
P\rightarrow M
for an arbitrary
G
-bundle (e.g., Dirac monopole in electromagnetism,
instantons in Yang-Mills theory), do
not
follow this description. In particular, the description of a
single
gauge field
A

as an object
living on spacetime
82
82

82

As opposed to its equivalent description of a
global

1
-form

\tilde{A}\in\Omega^{1}(P,\mathfrak{g})
on the corresponding principal
G
-bundle
P\rightarrow M
.

M
(with respect to a good open cover

\mathop{\text{\smash{\raisebox{-9.03763pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\prod$}}}\vphantom{$\prod$}}}_{i\in I}U_{i}\rightarrow M
) is given by a pair of families of

(a)
locally defined fields
\big\{A_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{i},\mathfrak{g})\big\}_{i\in I}

and

(b)
locally defined ‘‘transition maps’’
83
83

83

Corresponding to the isomorphisms between two different
trivializations of
P\rightarrow M
over
U_{i}
and
U_{j}
.

\big\{g_{ij}\in C^{\infty}(U_{ij},G)\big\}_{i,j\in I}
on the intersections
U_{ij}=U_{i}\cap U_{j}
such that

A_{j}=g_{ji}\cdot A_{i}\cdot g_{ji}^{-1}+g_{ji}\cdot\mathrm{d}g_{ji}^{-1}\quad\mathrm{and}\quad g_{ij}=g_{ik}\cdot g_{kj}

(171)

on overlaps
U_{ij}
and triple overlaps
U_{ijk}=U_{i}\cap U_{j}\cap U_{k}
, respectively. In other words, the collection of all
G
-gauge fields
on
M
does not form a (petit) mapping/section sheaf of Rem.
2.14
. Instead, they may be identified with an appropriate
mapping space construction in a higher categorical setting, via maps into a classifying moduli stack

M\rightarrow\mathbf{B}G_{\mathrm{conn}}
(
202
), hence forming a (petit)
mapping stack
on
M
!

One notices that the “transformations” relating the
\big\{A_{i}\in\Omega^{1}(U_{i},\mathfrak{g})\big\}
via the locally defined transition maps

\big\{g_{ij}\in C^{\infty}(U_{ij},G)\big\}_{i,j\in I}
have the same form as the action of (locally defined)
gauge transformations

in the case of the trivial topological sector. However, these transition maps are
data
entering the definition of the field configuration.
They are
not
to be viewed as redundancies and one should
not
quotient by them. Indeed, if one defines instead the field space to
be
equivalence classes

[A_{i}]
of locally defined forms by such transformations, then the information about the non-trivial topological
sectors is lost. In particular, if these (locally defined) transition maps were merely redundancies, then all gauge fields would be necessarily
equivalent to a globally defined
A\in\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g})
.

The actual gauge equivalences that may be partially viewed as redundancies in the vein of
(i)
are instead parametrized by locally defined
families
84
84

84

In contrast to transition the transition maps, these are defined locally on the open subsets
U_{i}
, rather than the
intersections
U_{ij}
.

\big\{\lambda_{i}\in C^{\infty}(U_{i},G)\big\}_{i\in I}
acting on any gauge field
85
85

85

Corresponding to pulling
back the connection on the total space
P\rightarrow M
along a principal
G
-bundle
automorphism
\bar{P}\rightarrow P
over
M
.

A=\big(\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I},\,\{g_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}\big)
by

\displaystyle\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\{\lambda_{i}^{-1}\cdot A_{i}\cdot\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{i}^{-1}\cdot\mathrm{d}\lambda_{i}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\{g_{ij}\}_{i,j\in I}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\{\lambda^{-1}_{i}\cdot g_{ij}\cdot\lambda_{j}\}_{i,j\in I}\,.

In fact, these natural automorphisms of the full nonperturbative field space consisting of
G
-gauge fields of arbitrary topological sectors
are automatically encoded in the (smooth) mapping stack/groupoid construction
[M,\mathbf{B}G_{\mathrm{conn}}]
as homotopies/higher morphisms
between the two gauge field configurations
[
FSS12
]
[
FSS13
]
[
FSS14
]
, as we will make further explicit in
future installments of this series.

7
Presymplectic structure of local field theories

The setting of smooth sets is the natural arena to make fully rigorous the observation of
[
Zu86
]
(considerably expanded in
[
DF99
]
),
that the usual manipulations of classical
local
field theory (for example as described in the previous sections) may be
concisely expressed via the bicomplex of
local
differential forms on the product ‘smooth space’
\mathcal{F}\times M
of off-shell fields
and the spacetime, which arises as the ‘pullback’ of the variational bicomplex on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
.

The point is that in our current context
the spaces in question
exist
as smooth sets. There is a natural notion of a
smooth
tangent bundle on
\mathcal{F}\times M
,
and hence differential forms on it, whereby one can genuinely pullback forms on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
via the
smooth prolongated
evaluation
map
\mathrm{ev}\circ(j^{\infty}\times\mathrm{id}_{M}):\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
, hence identifying a subset of differential forms with
an induced bi-complex structure. This recovers the bicomplex of local forms as implicitly used in
[
Zu86
]
and
[
DF99
]
.
For any local field theory
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
, the main additional insight of this formalism is the study of the induced presymplectic

(d-1,2)
-form
\omega_{\mathcal{L}}
on
\mathcal{F}\times M
, its restriction to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\times M
and its transgression to an on-shell
presymplectic
2
-form
\int_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
upon integration along an appropriate submanifold, yielding
the
covariant phase space
of a local field theory as a presymplectic smooth set.

As previously, we rigorously define the relevant structures within the category of smooth sets, while also describing the exact
correspondence with the formulas appearing in the physics literature.
86
86

86

A similar formalization is described in
[
Blo23
]
,
which however takes place in ‘pro-diffeological’ spaces. And hence in turn pro-smooth sets, rather than actual smooth sets.
This is not ideal and has its limitations – see Rem.
3.8
. Further discrepancies in definitions and results
in comparison to our approach will be noted throughout this section. However, a discussion of the presymplectic and induced
Poisson structures does not appear therein.
Our presentation includes a detailed discussion of the
off-shell presymplectic current of field theories, and the induced
bracket structures of Noether and Hamiltonian local currents, which in good situations restricts to the on-shell product
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\times M
.
Finally, by transgressing the presymplectic current to an actual presymplectic
2
-form on
\mathcal{F}
, we describe the analogous induced Poisson
bracket on Hamiltonian functionals. In good situations, this restricts to the
covariant Poisson bracket
of Hamiltonian
functionals on the space on-shell fields
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
, i.e., the Poisson bracket corresponding to the presymplectic structure
of the covariant phase space.

In the mathematical physics literature, early accounts for the covariant phase space approach include

[
So77
]
[
Ki73
]
[
KS76
]

[
Ga72
]
[
GK74
]
[
KT79
]
.
It was rediscovered in
[
Wi86
]
[
Zu86
]
, see also
[
CW87
]
.
Detailed historical accounts, with relevant references, of the development of the covariant
phase space formalism can be found in
[
Gi23
]
and
[
Ka14
]
.
In the latter, the approach is via the infinite jet bundle, and hence closer to ours, where furthermore
the relation with Peierls bracket (defined in
terms of Green functions, when these exist) is explained in detail.

7.1
The local bicomplex and its Cartan calculus

We start off by defining the tangent bundle on the product smooth set
87
87

87

In this final section, we omit reference
to the fully faithful Yoneda embedding symbols. We hope that, by now, the reader should be able to tell from the ambient
context which category the objects we refer to live in (
\mathrm{SmthMfd}
,
\mathrm{LocProMan}
or
\mathrm{SmthSet}
).

\mathcal{F}\times M\in\mathrm{SmthSet}
to be

T(\mathcal{F}\times M):=T\mathcal{F}\times TM\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636pt\mathrm{SmthSet}\,,

and so at the level of points
T_{(\phi,p)}(\mathcal{F}\times M)=T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}\times T_{p}M=\Gamma_{M}(\phi^{*}VF)\times T_{p}M\subset T(\mathcal{F}\times M)(*)
.
The intuition is that both tangent vectors on M and
\mathcal{F}
are given by ‘infinitesimal curves’ in
M
and
\mathcal{F}
respectively, and thus
so should tangent vectors on
\mathcal{F}\times M
. This intuition will, once again, become a constructive definition in the synthetic
sense – recovering the above (see
[
GS24
]
). Viewing
\mathcal{F}\times M
as a
trivial
bundle over spacetime

\textstyle{\mathcal{F}\times M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{pr}_{2}}
\textstyle{M\,,}

we may think of the (by definition) splitting of the tangent bundle
T(\mathcal{F}\times M)
as a horizontal splitting along the projection to
M
.
In other words,

\displaystyle V(\mathcal{F}\times M)\times_{\mathcal{F}\times M}H(\mathcal{F}\times M):=(T\mathcal{F}\times M)\times_{\mathcal{F}\times M}(\mathcal{F}\times TM)\cong T\mathcal{F}\times TM\,,

(172)

and so
T\mathcal{F}\times M\hookrightarrow T\mathcal{F}\times TM
is the
vertical
subbundle while
\mathcal{F}\times TM\hookrightarrow T\mathcal{F}\times TM
is the
horizontal
subbundle.

By definition, differential
m
-forms on both the spacetime manifold
M
and the infinite jet bundle
J^{\infty}_{M}F
(Def.
4.13
)
are given by fiber-wise linear bundle maps out of their tangent bundle (in
\mathrm{SmthSet}
). We define differential forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M

in the same vein and in line with differential forms on
\mathcal{F}
from Def.
2.27
.

Definition 7.1

(
Forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
).

The set of differential m-forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
is defined as

\displaystyle\Omega^{m}(\mathcal{F}\times M):=\mathrm{Hom}^{\mathrm{fib.lin.an.}}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(T^{\times m}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\,,\,\mathbbm{R}\big)\,,

(173)

i.e., smooth real-valued, fiber-wise linear antisymmetric maps with respect to the fiber-wise linear structure on the
m
-fold fiber product

T^{\times m}(\mathcal{F}\times M):=T(\mathcal{F}\times M)\times_{\mathcal{F}\times M}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{F}\times M}T(\mathcal{F}\times M)

of the tangent bundle over the
\mathcal{F}\times M
.

As usual, the collection of differential forms of all degrees forms a graded
\mathbbm{R}
-vector space

\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\times M):=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbbm{N}}\Omega^{m}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\,.

Furthermore, it is straightforward to define what a
horizontal
and
vertical
form on
\mathcal{F}\times M
should be.
For instance, as with the infinite jet bundle (Def.
5.1
), a horizontal
1
-form
\omega
on
\mathcal{F}\times M

is one that vanishes under the restriction

\displaystyle\omega|_{V(\mathcal{F}\times M)}:T\mathcal{F}\times M\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}\times TM\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}\;,

(174)

and analogously for vertical
1
-forms, and even further for horizontal / vertical
m
-forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
. At this point,
the development of a bi-complex structure and, furthermore, a corresponding Cartan calculus on
\mathcal{F}\times M
reaches several stumbling blocks:

Remark 7.2

(
Caveats with the ‘bicomplex of
\mathcal{F}\times M
’).

Both
[
Zu86
]
and
[
DF99
]

seem to assume (albeit unnecessarily) the existence of a bi-complex structure on the set of
all
differential forms
88
88

88

Although
they do not explicitly define what this set consists of. We presume they mean a version of our description.

\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
,
along with a Cartan Calculus with respect to vector fields on
\mathcal{F}\times M
. However, the (potential) existence of this structure suffers from the following ambiguities:

(a)
Even though there is a notion of horizontal/vertical
1
-forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
, it is not obvious that
every

1
-form
\omega
on
\mathcal{F}\times M
splits as

\omega\overset{?}{=}\omega_{V}+\omega_{H}\,.

Recall, the reason this holds for finite-dimensional manifolds and the infinite jet bundle is that
1
-forms, in these cases, are in 1-1 correspondence with
maps of modules of vector fields (Rem.
4.12
). This in turn uses the paracompact structure of the underlying (Fréchet) manifolds to
extend tangent vectors on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
to vector fields. This is not at all obvious for the case of the field space
\mathcal{F}
, and in turn for
\mathcal{F}\times M
. In other words,
this seems to be a non-trivial obstacle towards defining the bigrading on
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
. A potential proof that might overcome this is sketched in Rem.
7.39
. Nevertheless, there is a further crucial issue.

(b)
There is no obvious definition for a ‘de Rham differential’

\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{F}}:\Omega^{m}(\mathcal{F})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{m+1}(\mathcal{F})\,.

Nevertheless, we note that for any vector field
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}
there is a natural contraction / interior product operation
89
89

89

This extends to higher
degrees. For
m=2
, the precomposition
T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id},\mathcal{Z})}T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}T\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
defines a
1
-form by noting the canonical
isomophism
T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{F}\cong T\mathcal{F}
as a fiber product over
\mathcal{F}
.

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}:\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\longmapsto\omega\circ\mathcal{Z}\,,

which is however insufficient for a Cartan calculus in the absence of a differential. Naturally, this is directly related to the observations
of Rem.
2.18
and Eq. (
33
), since the would-be differential would necessarily define
a derivation / Lie derivative
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}=\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{F}}
on
C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
. The same statements follow through for the case of
\mathcal{F}\times M
,
even without the further demand of an underlying bi-grading structure.

(c)
We note that the problems of
(a), (b)
are evaded if one instead takes as a definition of forms on
\mathcal{F}
that of the classifying
space
\mathbold{\Omega}^{m}_{\mathrm{dR}}
(Def.
2.31
). As explained therein, a de Rham differential exists for this version of forms on
\mathcal{F}
,
and similarly for those on
\mathcal{F}\times M
. In fact, one can see that a natural bicomplex structure exists
90
90

90

To see this, recall that

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathcal{F}\times M,\,\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}\big(\mathcal{F},\,[M,\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}]\big)

by the internal hom property. Evaluating on test probes, the mapping space on the right is canonically isomorphic to

\big(y(\mathbbm{R})\hat{\otimes}\Omega^{1}(M)\big)\bigoplus\big(\mathbold{\Omega}^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}\hat{\otimes}C^{\infty}(M)\big)
, hence splitting
a de Rham
1
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
as
\omega=\omega^{1,0}+\omega^{0,1}
. This generalizes to de Rham m-forms, and hence splitting the
de Rham differential (Def.
2.32
) as
\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{\mathcal{F}}+\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{dR}}^{M}
, inducing a bi-complex structure.

on
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{dR}}(\mathcal{F}\times M):=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{SmthSet}}(\mathcal{F}\times M,\,\mathbold{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{dR}})
. However, in the
setting of smooth sets the relation of the (classifying) de Rham forms with those of Def.
7.1
is unclear.
91
91

91

As we have hinted
before in Rem.
2.33
, the situation is different in the setting of infinitesimally
thickened smooth sets where the two notions will, indeed, be intricately related.

Furthermore, there is no obvious interior product operation, which once
again obstructs the construction of a Cartan calculus.

The resolution, from our perspective, is that
local
classical field theory – and hence all of the constructions and arguments
of
[
Zu86
]
[
DF99
]
– only requires the existence of a bi-complex structure (and a corresponding Cartan Calculus) on the subset
of
local
forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
(along with the set of local vector fields), hence completely bypassing the above conundrum.
This
local bicomplex
does
exist
within our context of smooth sets, and does indeed rigorously recover the statements and
formulas of the above references.
To that end, recall the smooth evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}:\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(F)\times M\rightarrow F

from Eq. (
24
). There is an analogous smooth evaluation map, which we denote by the same symbol,

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}:\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(J^{\infty}F)\times M

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F

\displaystyle(\tilde{\phi},p)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\tilde{\phi}(p)\,,

and similarly for higher plots. Precomposing along the smooth jet prolongation
j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(J^{\infty}F)
,
we may define the smooth prolongated evaluation map with values in
J^{\infty}_{M}F
.

Definition 7.3

(
Prolongated evaluation).

The
prolongated evaluation map

\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
is defined as the composition of maps of smooth sets

\displaystyle\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\times M\xlongrightarrow{\hskip 8.19447pt(j^{\infty},\,\mathrm{id}_{M})\hskip 8.19447pt}\mathbold{\Gamma}_{M}(J^{\infty}F)\times M\xlongrightarrow{\;\;\mathrm{ev}\;\;}J^{\infty}_{M}F\,.

Explicitly, at the level of
*
-plots this is given by

\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}(\phi,p)=j^{\infty}\phi(p)\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636ptJ^{\infty}_{M}F\,,

and similarly for higher plots.

At this point, we note that both the source
\mathcal{F}\times M
and target
J^{\infty}_{M}F
of the prolongated evaluation consist of smooth spaces with a well-defined
tangent bundle, in terms of infinitesimal curves.
92
92

92

In
[
GS24
]
all of the mentioned tangent bundles will be special instances of the synthetic tangent
bundle construction, making the intuition of infinitesimal curves precise. The pushforward map motivated below will then follow naturally as the synthetic
pushforward of
\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}
.
Moreover, by Lem.
2.17
, we can represent any tangent vector

(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi},X_{p})\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}\times T_{p}M\subset T\mathcal{F}\times TM
by a line plot
(\phi_{t},p_{t})\in\big(\mathcal{F}\times M\big)(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t})
starting at
(\phi,p)
.
Since tangent vectors on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
are also represented by line plots (Lem.
4.2
), this suggests the existence of
a pushforward map along
\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}
. Indeed, we may calculate the derivative of
\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}\circ(\phi_{t},p_{t}):\mathbbm{R}^{1}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
at
t=0
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\big(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}(\phi_{t},p_{t})\big)|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\big(j^{\infty}\phi_{t}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}},p_{t})\big)|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}(j^{\infty}\phi_{t})|_{t=0}(p_{t=0})+\partial_{t}\big(j^{\infty}\phi_{t=0}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}},p_{t})\big)|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}(j^{\infty}\phi_{t})|_{t=0}(p)+(\mathrm{d}j^{\infty}\phi)_{p}(\partial_{t}p_{t}|_{t=0})

\displaystyle=j^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}(p)+(\mathrm{d}j^{\infty}\phi)_{p}X_{p}\,,

where the derivative is computed as in Lem.
4.2
and Ex.
4.7
,
hence via the corresponding compatible family
\{j^{k}\phi_{t}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}},p_{t}):\mathbbm{R}^{1}\rightarrow J^{k}_{M}F\}_{k\in\mathbbm{N}}
, whereby the
chain rule applies – as done implicitly in the second equality. The latter equalities show that the derivative naturally combines the two
previously introduced pushforward maps: The ‘vertical pushforward’ of Eq. (
75
) via the prolongation of the tangent
vector
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}
at
\phi\in\mathcal{F}
evaluated at
p\in M

j^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}(p)\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636ptV_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}J^{\infty}_{M}F\,,

and the ‘horizontal pushforward’ (horizontal lift) of Eq. (
86
) via the differential of the prolongation
of
\phi\in\mathcal{F}
at
p\in M

(\mathrm{d}j^{\infty}\phi)_{p}X_{p}\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636ptH_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}J^{\infty}_{M}F\,.

The resulting tangent vector on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
depends only on the tangent vectors
(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi},X_{p})
, and so we may disregard the representative
line-plots and define the pushforward of
\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}
directly as a map of tangent vectors.

Definition 7.4

(
Pushforward of prolongated evaluation).

The
pushforward
of the prolongated evaluation
\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}:T\mathcal{F}\times TM

\displaystyle\;\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\;TJ^{\infty}_{M}F

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi},X_{p})

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;j^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}(p)+(\mathrm{d}j^{\infty}\phi)_{p}X_{p}\,,

and similarly for higher plots.

In a compatible coordinate chart for
J^{\infty}_{M}F
around
p\in M
where
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}=\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{a}}\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}

and
X_{p}=X^{\mu}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}|_{p}\in T_{p}M
, the pushforward is given by (see maps (
75
)
and (
86
))

\displaystyle\bigg(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{a}},\,X^{\mu}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\Big|_{p}\bigg)\;\;\longmapsto\;\;\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{a}}{\partial x^{I}}(p)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{a}_{I}}\Big|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}+X^{\mu}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}}\Big|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}+\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial\phi^{a}}{\partial x^{I+\mu}}(p)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{a}_{I}}\Big|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}\bigg)\,.

(175)

We highlight that, by construction, the pushforward
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}
respects the natural splitting of the tangent bundles
T\mathcal{F}\times TM

from Eq. (
172
), and that of
TJ^{\infty}_{M}F
from Cor.
4.9
, as bundles over
M

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 61.83055pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-61.83055pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{V(\mathcal{F}\times M)\times_{\mathcal{F}\times M}H(\mathcal{F}\times M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 96.8306pt\raise-8.90413pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 80.50365pt\raise 5.5853pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\hskip 8.19447pt\,\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 148.6223pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{VJ^{\infty}_{M}F\times_{J^{\infty}_{M}F}HJ^{\infty}_{M}F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 96.8306pt\raise-9.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{M}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 195.01434pt\raise-9.66663pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The idea now is to define the subset of
local forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
by pulling back forms in the variational bi-complex on

J^{\infty}_{M}F
via
e_{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
, by which we mean precomposing differential forms with the
pushforward map on tangent vectors. In other words, the
pullback

local
form
(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

of a differential form
\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(J^{\infty}_{M}F)
is defined by the composition

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega:T\mathcal{F}\times TM\xlongrightarrow{\;\;\mathrm{d}\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}\;\;}J^{\infty}_{M}F\xlongrightarrow{\;\;\omega\;\;}\mathbbm{R}\,.

(176)

Since forms on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
do have a well-defined bigrading, and the pushforward map respects the respective splittings,
it follows that the image of the map of
\mathbbm{R}
-vector spaces

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}:\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(J^{\infty}_{M}F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\,.

induces a bi-grading – whose horizontal and vertical components coincide with the corresponding notions defined (
174
)
directly on
T\mathcal{F}\times TM
. Thus, it follows that the vertical and horizontal differentials on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
induce two (anti-commuting)
differentials on the image of
(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}
. In total, the pullback identifies a
bi-complex structure
on the subspace of

local forms
on the smooth set
\mathcal{F}\times M
.

Definition 7.5

(
Bicomplex of local forms).

The
bicomplex of local forms
on
\mathcal{F}\times M
is defined as the image of the variational bicomplex on
J^{\infty}_{M}F
under
the pullback map
(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}
. That is,

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M):=\mathrm{Im}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\,:\,\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(J^{\infty}_{M}F)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\big)\quad\subset\;\;\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\,,

equipped with the induced horizontal and vertical differentials

\mathrm{d}_{M}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big):=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}(\mathrm{d}_{H}\omega)\,,\hskip 56.9055pt\delta\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big)\equiv\mathrm{d}_{\mathcal{F}}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big):=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}(\mathrm{d}_{V}\omega)\,,

respectively.

By construction, the local bi-complex is a module over local functions, or equivalently local
0
-forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
, i.e.,

C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\cong\Omega^{0,0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
. It is instructive to spell out the form of the local
bicomplex in local coordinates, which will make contact with the formulas of
[
DF99
]
. To that end, for any 1-form

\omega=\omega_{H}+\omega_{V}=(\omega_{H})_{\mu}\cdot\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}+\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\cdot\mathrm{d}_{V}u^{a}_{I}
on
J^{\infty}_{M}F

and any tangent vector
(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi},X_{p})\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}\times T_{p}M
, we may compute

\displaystyle\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big)|_{(\phi,p)}(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi},X_{p})

\displaystyle=\omega|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}\Big((\mathrm{d}\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})_{(\phi,p)}\big(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi},p\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=(\omega_{H}+\omega_{V})|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}\big(j^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}(p)+(dj^{\infty}\phi)_{p}X_{p}\big)

(177)

\displaystyle=\omega_{V}|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}\big(j^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}(p)\big)+\omega_{H}|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}\big((\mathrm{d}j^{\infty}\phi)_{p}X_{p}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}\big(\omega_{V}\big)_{a}^{I}(j^{\infty}_{p}\phi)\cdot\frac{\partial\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{a}}{\partial x^{I}}(p)+\big(\omega_{H}\big)_{\mu}(j^{\infty}_{p}\phi)\cdot X^{\mu}\,,

where in the third equality we used the vertical/horizontal properties of the two tangent vectors, respectively, and finally used their coordinate form (
175
). Note that, even though
e^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
might not be
surjective
93
93

93

In more detail, as per Rem.
2.14
, this is really a map of petit sheaves (and hence the pullback too) – i.e.,
over each
U\subset M
. Regardless of the (non)-existence of global sections, the restricted map
ev^{\infty}|_{U}:\Gamma_{U}(F)\times U\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{U}F

might still not be surjective for all
U\subset M
, and the comment applies for each open neighborhood. It is, however, an epimorphism as a map of (petit)
sheaves – i.e., surjective on stalks.
or submersive (surjective on tangent spaces), the differentials of a local form are well-defined, i.e., independent of the chosen representative.
This follows since pullback
(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega
local form depends manifestly only the values of
\omega\in J^{\infty}_{M}F
along the image
of
\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}
(see (

Standard notation for the local bicomplex.

Recalling the abuse of notation from (
147
),
we may denote the vertical tangent vector
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}=\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{a}}\,\in\,T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}=\Gamma_{M}(\phi^{*}VF)
as

\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}=\mathcal{Z}^{a}\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{a}}\Big|_{(\phi,p)}\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636ptT_{\phi}\mathcal{F}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}\times T_{p}M\,.

Following this trend, the pullback of a vertical
1
-form
\omega_{V}=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\cdot\mathrm{d}_{V}u^{a}_{I}=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\big(x^{\mu},\{u^{b}_{J}\}_{|J|\leq k}\big)\cdot\mathrm{d}_{V}u^{a}_{I}
may be denoted as

\displaystyle\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega_{V}\big):

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\circ\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}\cdot(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{V}u^{a}_{I}

(178)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k}\big)\cdot\delta(\partial_{I}\phi^{a})\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636pt\Omega^{0,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

with the implicit understanding that the notation here
94
94

94

The abuse of notation would be slightly less confusing if we used the notation
\hat{\phi}^{a}_{I}
from Ex.
3.22
. We comply with the standard physics literature notation as in
[
DF99
]
, to make direct contact with the formulas therein.

means
\partial_{I}\phi^{a}:=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}u^{a}_{I}=u^{a}_{I}\circ\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
, and so

\delta(\partial_{I}\phi^{a})\equiv\delta(u^{a}_{I}\circ\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}):=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{V}u^{a}_{I}\,.

Under this abuse of notation, the rigorous calculation of (
7.1
) justifies the following useful (formal) manipulation from
[
DF99
]

\displaystyle\delta(\partial_{I}\phi^{a})\big|_{\phi,p}\bigg(\mathcal{Z}^{b}_{\phi}\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{b}}\Big|_{(\phi,p)}\bigg)=\partial_{I}\bigg(\delta\phi^{a}\Big(\mathcal{Z}^{b}_{\phi}\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{b}}\Big)\!\bigg)(p)=\partial_{I}\mathcal{Z}^{a}_{\phi}(p)=\frac{\partial\mathcal{Z}^{a}_{\phi}}{\partial x^{I}}(p)\,,

(179)

where, as explained, the partial derivative symbol on the left-hand side is a formal symbol, while on the right-hand
side it is an actual derivative. In other words, with the caveats and abuse of notation mentioned, the ‘vertical differential commutes with partial derivatives’

\displaystyle``\,\delta(\partial_{I}\phi^{a})=\partial_{I}(\delta\phi^{a})\,"\,,

which is precisely how these symbols are usefully manipulated in explicit calculations appearing in the literature.

Along the same lines, given a smooth function
f=f\big(x,\{u^{b}_{J}\}_{|J|\leq k}\big)\in C^{\infty}(J^{\infty}_{M}F)
with induced local function
(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}f\in\Omega^{0,0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
, again by (
7.1
), the corresponding local vertical 1-form may be computed and denoted as

\displaystyle\delta\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}f):

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{V}f=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial f}{\partial u^{a}_{I}}\circ\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}\cdot(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{V}u^{a}_{I}

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}\frac{\delta f\big(x,\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k}\big)}{\delta(\partial_{I}\phi^{a})}\cdot\delta(\partial_{I}\phi^{a})

(180)

On the other hand, the corresponding local horizontal 1-form is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{M}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}f):

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{H}f=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}(D_{\mu}f\cdot\mathrm{d}_{H}x^{\mu})=D_{\mu}f\circ\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}\cdot(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{H}x^{\mu}\,,

which at any point
(\phi,p)\in\mathcal{F}\times M
, again by (
7.1
), is further given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{M}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}f)|_{(\phi,p)}

\displaystyle=D_{\mu}f(j^{\infty}_{p}\phi)\cdot\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\big|_{p}=\Big(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{\mu}}\circ j^{\infty}\phi\Big)(p)\cdot\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}\Big|_{p}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}_{M}(f\circ j^{\infty}\phi)|_{p}

as a map
T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}\times T_{p}M\xrightarrow{\mathsf{pr}_{2}}T_{p}M\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
, where in the second equality we used the chain rule
(
4.10
).
This justifies the notation
\mathrm{d}_{M}
for the horizontal differential on
\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
– it is
indeed simply the de Rham differential along the spacetime
M
, computed at each fixed field configuration
\phi\in\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{M}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}f\big)=\frac{\partial f\big(x,\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k}\big)}{\partial x^{\mu}}\cdot\mathrm{d}x^{\mu}

(181)

In these terms, the compatibility of the differentials from Prop.
5.21
is then simply the
further pullback,

\iota_{\phi}^{*}=\mathrm{ev}_{\phi}\circ\mathsf{pr}_{H}:\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet,0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(M)

for any

\iota_{\phi}^{*}(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{H}f=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}\circ\iota_{\phi})^{*}\mathrm{d}_{H}f=(j^{\infty}\phi)^{*}\mathrm{d}_{H}f=\mathrm{d}_{M}(f\circ j^{\infty}\phi)\,.

This discussion naturally generalizes to arbitrary local

with the action of the vertical

Local Cartan calculus.

Recall for any (smooth) vector field
\mathcal{Z}:\mathcal{F}\times M\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}\times TM
, there is a natural interior product operation

(
Rem
7.2
(b)
)

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}:\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\longmapsto\omega\circ\mathcal{Z}\,,

since a differential form is, by definition, a smooth map
T\mathcal{F}\times TM\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
. As the notation suggests, there is no reason the resulting smooth
function
\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\omega=\omega\circ\mathcal{Z}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}\times M)=\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
should be
local
. Nevertheless, this is indeed the case for

local
vector fields
\mathcal{Z}=Z\circ j^{\infty}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
viewed as vector fields
on
97
97

97

Formally,
\mathcal{Z}=Z\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}
defines a smooth vector field on
\mathcal{F}\times M
by
(\phi,p)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\mathcal{Z}(\phi),0_{p})\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}\times T_{p}M
.
We will use the same notation for the resulting vector field on
\mathcal{F}\times M
.

\mathcal{F}\times M
constant along
M
, with vanishing horizontal components.
Indeed, one has

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big)=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}(\iota_{\mathsf{pr}Z}\omega)\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636pt\Omega^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

which can be seen via

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big)|_{(\phi,p)}

\displaystyle=\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\circ\mathcal{Z}\big)(\phi,p)=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega|_{(\phi,p)}(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi})

\displaystyle=\omega_{V}|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}\big(j^{\infty}(Z\circ j^{\infty}\phi)(p)\big)=\omega_{V}|_{j^{\infty}_{p}\phi}\big(\mathsf{pr}Z\circ j^{\infty}\phi(p)\big)

(183)

\displaystyle=\omega_{V}(\mathsf{pr}Z)\circ\mathrm{ev}^{\infty}(\phi,p)=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}(\iota_{\mathsf{pr}Z}\omega)(\phi,p)\,,

where the second line follows by (
7.1
), with the identification
j^{\infty}(Z\circ j^{\infty}\phi)=\mathsf{pr}Z\circ j^{\infty}\phi
given, for instance, by the local descriptions (
75
) and (
150
). Using the standard notation
\mathcal{Z}=Z^{c}\big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k^{\prime}}\big)\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{c}}
of (
147
)
and
(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k}\big)\cdot\delta(\partial_{I}\phi^{a}\big)
of (
178
), along with the abuse of (
179
), this may be also calculated as

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\big((\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\Big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k}\Big)\cdot\delta(\partial_{I}\phi^{a})\bigg(Z^{c}\Big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k^{\prime}}\Big)\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{c}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\Big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k}\Big)\cdot\partial_{I}\bigg(Z^{c}\Big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k^{\prime}}\Big)\cdot\delta\phi^{a}\Big(\frac{\delta}{\delta\phi^{c}}\Big)\!\bigg)

(184)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|I|=0}^{\infty}(\omega_{V})_{a}^{I}\Big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k}\Big)\cdot\frac{\partial Z^{a}\big(x^{\mu},\{\partial_{J}\phi^{b}\}_{|J|\leq k^{\prime}}\big)}{\partial x^{I}}\,,

which is the form in which it appears in
[

]
.

The interior product with respect to local vector fields extends to arbitrary local forms as a (graded)
derivation
98
98

98

Equivalently, for any
\omega\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
the contracted local
1
-form is
the map
\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\omega:=T\mathcal{F}\cong T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{(\mathrm{id},\mathcal{Z})}T\mathcal{F}\times_{\mathcal{F}}T\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{\omega}\mathbbm{R}
, and similarly for higher forms.

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}:\Omega^{p,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\rightarrow\Omega^{p,q-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\,,

and so given locally as above by acting on the pullback local forms on their presentation of (
182
).
In particular, for a horizontal local form

\mathcal{P}=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}P\in\Omega_{\mathrm{loc}}^{p,0}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
, or equivalently
\mathcal{P}=P\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\Omega^{p}_{\mathrm{Vert}}(M)
,
the contraction of its vertical differential
\delta\mathcal{P}\in\Omega^{p,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
along local vector fields immediately
recovers exactly the action of Def.
6.8

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\delta\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{P})\hskip 14.22636pt\in\hskip 14.22636pt\Omega^{d,0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\,,

which may be seen at the abstract level by (
7.1
), or via the coordinate formulas (
7.1
). This suggests that the action of local vector fields on
p
-form currents is a kind of Lie derivative on
\mathcal{F}\times M
.

Definition 7.6

(
Lie derivative along local vector field).

Let
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
be any local vector field. The Lie derivative along
\mathcal{Z}
is defined by
\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}:=[\iota_{\mathcal{Z}},\delta]
,
that is

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}:\Omega^{p,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

\displaystyle\;\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\;\Omega^{p,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

\displaystyle(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\big(\delta(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big)+\delta\big(\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega\big)\,.

Expanding the right-hand side using Def.
7.5
and (
7.1
), this is equivalently

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}(\iota_{\mathsf{pr}Z}\mathrm{d}_{V}\omega+\mathrm{d}_{V}\iota_{\mathsf{pr}Z}\omega)=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathsf{pr}Z}\omega)\,.

We could have used the total differential
\delta+\mathrm{d}_{M}
to define the Lie derivative, but this is redundant since the interior product
of vertical local vector fields on
\mathcal{F}\times M
commutes with
\mathrm{d}_{M}
,

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{d}_{M}=-\mathrm{d}_{M}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\,,

as can be seen abstractly, or by the local coordinate reprentations. More generally, we have the following result.

Proposition 7.7

(
Local Cartan calculus).

The Lie algebra of local vector fields
\big(\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}),\,[-,-])
(Def.
6.7
) acts on the local bicomplex

\big(\Omega^{\bullet,\bullet}(\mathcal{F}\times M),\,\delta,\mathrm{d}_{M}\,\big)
via the Lie derivative (Def.
7.6
)
and satisfies

\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}\,\mathrm{d}_{M}=\mathrm{d}_{M}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}\,,\hskip 56.9055pt\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}\,\delta=\delta\,\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}\,.

Proof.

This follows essentially by pulling back the relations of Cor.
6.2
. It can also be deduced directly using the
local descriptions of (
178
), (
181
), (
182
), and (
7.1
),
all of which have been rigorously justified.
∎

This local Cartan Calculus is essentially the core machinery in the descriptions of
[
Zu86
]
and
[
DF99
]
, here defined in full
detail with
\mathrm{SmthSet}
. It allows us to further explain the notation in
section

6.1
,
and also to lift the proofs of statements therein to proofs computed directly on
\mathcal{F}\times M
, as usually (implicitly) practiced in
the physics literature. For instance, the ‘infinitesimal transformation of a field’ notation of (
147
)
is rigorously identified as the contraction

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\delta\phi^{a}\equiv\delta_{\mathcal{Z}}\phi^{a}\,,

where now
\delta\phi^{a}
is really the vertical differential of the (locally defined) smooth function
\phi^{a}=u^{a}\circ ev^{\infty}

on
\mathcal{F}\times M
. Analogously, the corresponding abuse of notation from Def.
6.8
for the action
of local vector fields on currents is identified as the contraction

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\delta\mathcal{P}\equiv\delta_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{P}\,.

Similarly, the calculation that infinitesimal local symmetries of a local Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}\in\Omega^{d,0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

close as a subalgebra (
156
)
is equivalently expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{[\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}]}\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\,(\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\mathcal{L})-\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{L})=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}(\mathrm{d}_{M}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}})-\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}(\mathrm{d}_{M}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}_{M}(\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}-\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}})\,,

and, similarly, the proof of Noether’s First Theorem (Prop.
6.14
) as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{M}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Z}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}_{M}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}}+\mathrm{d}_{M}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\theta_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{L}-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{d}_{M}\theta_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\delta\mathcal{L}-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{d}_{M}\theta_{\mathcal{L}}=\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}+\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{d}_{M}\theta_{\mathcal{L}}-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathrm{d}_{M}\theta_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}\,,

where
\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}
here stands for the induced vertical 1-form on
\mathcal{F}\times M
given by

(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi},X_{p})\mapstochar\rightarrow\langle\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}(\phi),\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\rangle(p)
. Following the above, it immediately follows that the
conserved current
\mathcal{P}_{[\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}]}
corresponding to the Lie bracket
[\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}]\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})
of two local symmetries is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{[\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}]}=\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}-\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}+\iota_{[\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}]}\theta_{\mathcal{L}}\,.

(185)

Once again, we note that this actually defines a family of conserved currents, with the freedom to add
\mathrm{d}_{M}
-closed terms
to
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}},\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}
and
\mathrm{d}_{M}
-exact terms to
\theta_{\mathcal{L}}
. We will come back with a better characterization of the
right-hand side in terms of the conserved currents
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}
themselves via Def.
7.26
.

Expanding the above formulas in local coordinates along the lines of (
178
), (
181
),
(
182
), and (
7.1
), recovers verbatim those appearing in the physics literature,
thus rigorously fully justifying their validity as statements about
smooth
and
local
geometry on the full
smooth

space of fields
\mathcal{F}
and its smooth subspace of on-shell fields
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
.

Remark 7.8

(
Extended Local Cartan calculus).

(i)
At a purely mathematical level, an analogous discussion applies for a larger class of ‘local’ vector fields on
\mathcal{F}\times M
:

(a)

Decomposable vector fields of the form
\mathcal{Z}+X\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\times\mathcal{X}(M)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
are considered in
[
DF99
]
.
The corresponding Cartan calculus corresponds to two commuting Cartan calculi given by the local vertical of Lem.
7.7
,
along with that of the base manifold
\big([-,-]_{M},\mathrm{d}_{M},\iota\big)
. In particular, the Lie derivative in this case is the decomposed one

\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}+X}=[\iota_{\mathcal{Z}},\delta]+[\iota_{X},\mathrm{d}_{M}]\equiv\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}}+\mathbb{L}_{X}
.

(b)

It can be further (maximally) extended to include vector fields induced by general smooth bundle maps
J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow TF
,
whose prolongation splits (
[
An89
, Prop. 1.20]
) as the prolongation of an evolutionary vector field
Z:J^{\infty}M\rightarrow VF
,
and the horizontal lift of some bundle map
X:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow TM
over
M
. Thus the induced local vector fields are of the form

\mathcal{Z}+\mathcal{X}
on
\mathcal{F}\times M
, where
\mathcal{Z}
is a local vector field on
\mathcal{F}
, and
\mathcal{X}(\phi)
is a vector field on
M
for each
\phi\in\mathcal{F}
,
with a local but
not constant
dependence along
\mathcal{F}
. The corresponding larger, more complicated, Cartan Calculus on
\mathcal{F}\times M

is carefully spelled out in
[
De18
]
, where the vector fields in question are referred to as “
insular
”. In this case, the Lie
