. Therein, the discussion differs from ours since the total grading on the bicomplex of local forms is employed, and
Hamiltonian pairs are defined relative to the
(\delta+\mathrm{d}_{M})
-closed “Poincaré-Cartan”
\tilde{\omega}_{\mathcal{L}}:=\omega_{\mathcal{L}}-\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}
.

7.3
The covariant phase space, off-shell and on-shell Poisson brackets

The results of
section

7.2
, which take place on
\mathcal{F}\times M
and its smooth subspace
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\times M
,
may now be
transgressed
to statements about the actual field space
\mathcal{F}
and the subspace of on-shell fields
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
,
essentially by integrating the horizontal parts of the bi-graded forms involved over submanifolds of spacetime
M
.
We will use the notation
\varpi^{p,q}:=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\omega^{p,q}
.

Definition 7.34

(
Transgression).

Let
\Sigma^{p}\hookrightarrow M
be a compact oriented submanifold. The transgression of local
(p,q)
-forms on
\mathcal{F}\times M
to
q
-forms on
\mathcal{F}

\displaystyle\tau_{\Sigma^{p}}:\Omega^{p,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{q}(\mathcal{F})

is defined by

\displaystyle\tau_{\Sigma^{p}}\big(\varpi^{p,q}\big):(T\mathcal{F})^{\times q}

\displaystyle\;\;\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\quad\mathbbm{R}

\displaystyle(\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{1},\cdots,\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}^{q})

\displaystyle\;\;\longmapsto\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\big(\varpi^{p,q}\big)_{\phi}(\mathcal{Z}^{1}_{\phi},\cdots,\mathcal{Z}^{q}_{\phi},\cdots)\,,

and similarly for higher plots. We denote by

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc},\Sigma^{p}}(\mathcal{F})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})

the subalgebra of forms generated by transgressed local forms along
\Sigma^{p}
, and by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
generated by transgressed
local forms along arbitrary compact oriented submanifolds.

For
p=0
, transgression along
0
-dimensional submanifolds (points) is given simply by evaluation. Note that for
q=0
the transgression of
a local
p
-form current
\mathcal{P}
is simply its charge over
\Sigma^{p}

from Def.
3.19
.

Remark 7.35

(
Transgression over non-compact submanifolds).

For local
(p,0)
-forms, the transgression may be defined over noncompact submanifolds by restricting to the subspace of compactly supported field

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{cpt}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}
. However, for local
(p,q)
-forms with
q\geq 1
, the transgression may be defined over noncompact submanifolds
over the full field space, by restricting the domain of the transgressed map to be products of
T_{\mathrm{cpt}}\mathcal{F}\hookrightarrow T\mathcal{F}
, whose points
are tangent vectors to field space with compact support along the fibers. That is, a tangent vector
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\in T_{\mathrm{cpt},\phi}\mathcal{F}
is a
section
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\in\Gamma_{M}(\phi^{*}VF)
which maps to the zero element in each fiber
V_{\phi(x)}F
outside a compact
region
111
111

111

This can be further relaxed to tangent vectors with compact support only
after
restriction
along
\Sigma^{p}
.
of
M
. Thus, the transgression of a local
(p,q)
-form over a noncompact submanifold
\Sigma^{p}\hookrightarrow M
is,
strictly speaking not a
q
-form on
\mathcal{F}
, but instead a smooth map
(T_{\mathrm{cpt}}\mathcal{F})^{\times q}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
. Everything we say below applies
verbatim for such situations with minimal modifications, which we shall not make explicit.

Of special interest are the transgressions of the presymplectic potential current
\theta_{\mathcal{L}}:=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\theta_{L}
and, more importantly,
of the presymplectic current
\omega_{\mathcal{L}}=\delta\theta_{\mathcal{L}}
from Def.
7.10
.

Definition 7.36

(
Presymplectic potential and 2-form on field space).

The presymplectic potential
\Theta_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}
and presymplectic 2-form
\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}
of a Lagrangian field theory

(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
, with respect to a compact oriented submanifold
\Sigma^{d-1}
, are defined as the transgressions of the corresponding currents

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}:=\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(\theta_{\mathcal{L}})\quad:\quad T\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}

and

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}:=\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(\omega_{\mathcal{L}})\quad:\quad T\mathcal{F}\times T\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}\,,

respectively.

Now if
\Sigma^{d-1}
is without boundary, then both the presymplectic potential and
2
-form with respect to
\Sigma^{d-1}

are uniquely defined – in contrast with the corresponding currents which are only defined up to an addition of a
\mathrm{d}_{M}
-exact current.
In turn, these may be restricted/pulled back to forms on the space of on-shell fields
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
.
Equivalently, the currents may be first restricted to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\times M
and then transgressed via Def.
7.34
.
In particular, the presymplectic 2-form

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}:=\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(\omega_{\mathcal{L}})\big|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\equiv\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(\omega_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}})\quad:\quad T\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\times T\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}\times T\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}

depends only on the cobordism class of
\Sigma^{d-1}
.

Lemma 7.37

(
On-shell presymplectic 2-form and cobordism classes).

Let
B^{d}
be a cobordism between two compact oriented submanifolds
\partial B^{d}=\Sigma_{1}^{d-1}\mathop{\text{\smash{\raisebox{-9.03763pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\prod$}}}\vphantom{$\prod$}}}\Sigma_{2}^{d-1}
.
Then the corresponding transgressed on-shell presymplectic 2-forms coincide,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma_{1}^{d-1}}\big|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}=\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma_{2}^{d-1}}\big|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\quad\in\quad\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}})\,.

Proof.

By Lem.
7.19
, the presymplectic current is conserved on-shell
\mathrm{d}_{M}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}=0

and so for any cobordism
B^{d}
between two compact oriented submanifolds
\partial B^{d}=\Sigma_{1}^{d-1}\mathop{\text{\smash{\raisebox{-9.03763pt}{\scalebox{1}[-1]{$\prod$}}}\vphantom{$\prod$}}}\Sigma_{2}^{d-1}
, we have

\displaystyle 0=\int_{B^{d}}\mathrm{d}_{M}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}=\int_{\Sigma_{1}^{d-1}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}-\int_{\Sigma_{2}^{d-1}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\,,

and the result follows.
∎

In particular, this implies that if
\Sigma^{d-1}
is cobordant to the empty manifold, i.e., is homologically trivial in
M
,
then the corresponding on-shell
2
-form is trivial.
We have called
\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{F})
a ‘presymplectic’ 2-form on
\mathcal{F}
, and similarly its restriction on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
,
by virtue of
\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(\delta\omega_{\mathcal{L}})=0
. In order to witness it as a truly presymplectic
2
-form on
\mathcal{F}
, and that

\Theta_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}
is its potential, a notion of a differential on the algebra generated by transgressed forms

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\hookrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})
is required. Intuitively, and as implicitly practiced in the physics
literature, this is given by computing the vertical differential under the integral. The well-definiteness of this operation, however,
requires justification.

Lemma 7.38

(
Transgressed vertical differential).

The vertical differential
\delta:\Omega^{p,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)\rightarrow\Omega^{p,q+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
transgresses along any compact
oriented submanifold
\Sigma^{p}
to a well-defined map

\displaystyle\delta:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc},\Sigma^{p}}(\mathcal{F})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{\mathrm{loc},\Sigma^{p}}(\mathcal{F})

defined on transgressed local forms by

\delta\,\big(\tau_{\Sigma^{p}}(\varpi^{p,q})\big):=\tau_{\Sigma^{p}}\big(\delta(\varpi^{p,q})\big)\equiv\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta(\varpi^{p,q})\,,

and extends to the full algebra generated by transgressed local forms as a derivation
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
.

Proof.

The only ambiguous part of the definition is that there might be two different local
(p,q)
-forms
\varpi^{p,q}\neq\tilde{\varpi}^{p,q}:=(\mathrm{ev}^{\infty})^{*}\tilde{\omega}^{p,q}
such that their transgressions agree

\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\varpi^{p,q}=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,q}\,.

Thus for the transgressed vertical differential to be a well-defined map, it better be the case that the transgressions
of the vertical differentials
\delta\varpi^{p,q},\,\delta\tilde{\varpi}^{p,q}
also coincide

\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta\varpi^{p,q}=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta\tilde{\varpi}^{p,q}\,.

The proof of this follows by an inductive argument. Consider first case of two local
(p,0)
-form currents

\mathcal{P},\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\in\Omega^{p,0}(\mathcal{F}\times M)
with the same
charge
\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\mathcal{P}=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
along
\Sigma^{p}
. In this case, the result follows essentially
as with the variation of the charge of a Lagrangian (see proof of Prop.
5.31
).
Indeed, since the charge maps agree as maps of smooth sets, it follows that for any
1
-parameter family of fields

\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\mathcal{P}(\phi_{t})=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\tilde{\mathcal{P}}(\phi_{t})\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}_{t})\,.

The derivative evaluated at
t=0
gives

\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta\mathcal{P}_{\phi_{0}}(\partial_{t}\phi_{t}|_{{t}=0})=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\phi_{0}}(\partial_{t}\phi_{t}|_{{t}=0})

along the lines of Ex.
4.7
. Since any tangent vector on
\mathcal{F}
is represented by a line plot
(Lem.
2.17
), the result follows.

Next, consider the case of two local
(p,1)
-forms
\varpi^{p,1},\,\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}
such that the transgressions agree
\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\varpi^{p,1}=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}
. It follows that precomposing with local vector field
\mathcal{Z}_{1}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}
implies

\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\varpi^{p,1}=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}\,,

i.e., the equality of the transgressions of the local
(p,0)
-forms
\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\varpi^{p,1}
and
\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}
.
But we have already shown that for
(p,0)
-forms this further implies

\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\varpi^{p,1}=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}\,,

whereby precomposing with a further local vector field
\mathcal{Z}_{2}:\mathcal{F}\rightarrow T\mathcal{F}
gives

\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\delta\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\varpi^{p,1}=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\delta\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}\,,

or in terms of Lie derivatives

\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\varpi^{p,1}=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}\,.

Collecting the above, we may use the local Cartan calculus (Lem.
7.7
) under the integral to compute

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta\varpi^{p,1}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\varpi^{p,1}-\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\varpi^{p,1}-\iota_{[\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}]}\varpi^{p,1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}-\mathbb{L}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}-\iota_{[\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}]}\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Sigma^{p}}\delta\tilde{\varpi}^{p,1}(\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2})\,.

The case of transgressed local
(p,q)
-forms follows analogously, by induction.
∎

Remark 7.39

(
Caveat on transgressed vertical differential).

Strictly speaking, the proof of Lem.
7.38
only shows that the differential is well-defined on the maps
of local vector fields
\big(\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\big)^{\times q}\rightarrow C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
induced by transgressed local forms.
This is sufficient for all the uses of the transgressed vertical differential that will follow. To prove that it is actually well-defined
as a map of transgressed differential forms viewed as bundle maps
(T\mathcal{F})^{\times q}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}
, it is necessary to show that any
tangent vector
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\in T_{\phi}(\mathcal{F})
extends to a
local
vector field on
\mathcal{F}
.
We expect this is indeed true, but the proof requires Fréchet analytical details which we do not need to delve into here.
A sketch of the extension would be as follows: A tangent vector
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}
is a smooth bundle map
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}:M\rightarrow VF

covering
\phi
, and its jet prolongation (Ex.
4.7
) defines

j^{\infty}\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}:M\rightarrow VJ^{\infty}_{M}F
covering
j^{\infty}\phi:M\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
.
The image
j^{\infty}\phi(M)\hookrightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
should be a closed embedded Fréchet submanifold of the Hausdorff
and paracompact infinite jet bundle (Def.
50
), by essentially the same proof of the finite-dimensional manifold statement.
Thus the prolongation of
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}
is equivalently a section of the vector bundle
VJ^{\infty}_{M}F|_{j^{\infty}\phi(M)}
over
j^{\infty}\phi(M)
.
Composing with the pushforward of the projection
\mathrm{d}\pi^{\infty}_{0}:VJ^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow VF
gives a section

J^{\infty}_{M}F|_{j^{\infty}\phi(M)}\rightarrow(\pi^{\infty}_{0})^{*}VF|_{j^{\infty}\phi(M)}
. Assuming the result of extension of
vector bundle sections (see proof of Lem.
2.17
) over closed submanifolds extends to the
Hausdorff, paracompact case of the infinite jet bundle and a finite rank vector bundle over it, then there exists an extended
section
J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow(\pi^{\infty}_{0})^{*}VF
over
J^{\infty}_{M}F
. Equivalently, this is a bundle map

Z:J^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow VF
over
F
, i.e., an evolutionary vector field such that

\mathcal{Z}(\phi):=Z\circ j^{\infty}(\phi)=\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}
. Thus the induced local vector field

\mathcal{Z}:=Z\circ j^{\infty}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
extends
\mathcal{Z}_{\phi}\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}
.

Note that, by the proof of Prop.
5.39
, the criticality/on-shell condition (Def.
5.38
)
of a field
\phi
(and similarly of plots) corresponding to a
local
Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
, may be equivalently expressed as the joint
vanishing of the vertical transgressed differential

112
112

112

This is essentially the transgressed characterization from the footnote of the decomposition
(
5.1
).

\displaystyle\delta S_{\Sigma^{d}}|_{\phi}:T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}\,,

(200)

for all compact oriented submanifolds
\Sigma^{d}\hookrightarrow M
. This provides a further (equivalent) interpretation for the ‘variation’ symbol of local
Lagrangians/action functionals as used in the physics literature (cf. Rem.
5.41
(iii)).
Furthermore, using the transgressed vertical differential, it is
actually
the case that
\Theta_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})

is the presymplectic potential of
\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
, and that the latter is indeed presymplectic, i.e.,

\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}=\delta\Theta_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad\delta\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}=0\,.

In the case of a product spacetime with a
chosen
distinguished ‘space and time’ splitting
M^{d}\cong N^{d-1}\times\mathbbm{R}
,
as usually assumed in physical
theories, there is a uniquely determined
2
-form on the on-shell space of fields
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
, which makes it canonically into a presymplectic smooth set.

Definition 7.40

(
Covariant phase space).

Let
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
be a local classical field theory on a product
spacetime
M=N\times\mathbbm{R}
, where
N
is a compact oriented manifold. The
covariant phase space
of
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
is defined
as the presymplectic smooth set

(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\,,\Omega_{\mathcal{L}})\,,

where
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}:=\Omega_{\mathcal{L},N\times\{0\}}\big|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\equiv\Omega_{\mathcal{L},N\times\{t_{0}\}}\big|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
is the canonical transgression
of the on-shell presymplectic current by Lem.
7.37
.

Strictly speaking, if
N
has a non-trivial boundary, then the definition of
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}
actually depends on the choice of presymplectic current
\theta_{\mathcal{L}}
,
parametrized by the addition of
\mathrm{d}_{M}
-exact
(d-1,1)
-currents. Assuming the requirements of Rem.
6.6
are met, so that the local Cartan calculus descends to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\times M
, then the corresponding vertical differential also transgresses to
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}})
, by the same proof as in Lem.
7.38
, and hence
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}
is truly a presymplectic form
\delta\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}
on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
.

Of course, as we have hinted in the discussion of Lem.
7.23
, if infinitesimal gauge symmetries exist then the covariant
phase space cannot be symplectic, in that the 2-form
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}
is necessarily degenerate.

Lemma 7.41

(
Gauge symmetries imply degeneracy).

Let
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
be a local classical field theory on
N\times\mathbbm{R}
with some non-trivial (parametrized) infinitesimal gauge symmetries

\mathcal{R}_{(-)}:\Gamma_{M}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{F})
. Then the canonical on-shell presymplectic current
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}
is degenerate.

Proof.

Recall by Lem.
7.23
, for any gauge parameter
e\in\Gamma_{M}(E)

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{R}_{e}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}\big|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}=\mathrm{d}_{M}\tilde{\mathcal{T}}_{e}\big|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\quad\in\quad\Omega^{d-1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\times M)\,.

Recall, also by Prop.
7.17
, that the restriction
\mathcal{R}_{e}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
does
indeed define a vector field on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{R}_{e}(\phi)\in T_{\phi}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}})
for any
\phi\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}(*)
.
Choosing
e
to have non-trivial support along
N\times\{0\}
, then transgressing along
N\times\{0\}
gives

\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{R}_{e}(\phi),-)=0\quad:\quad T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}\,,

for all on-shell fields
\phi
and some
\mathcal{R}_{e}(\phi)\neq 0\in T_{\phi}\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
.
That is,
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}
is a degenerate
2
-form on
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
.
∎

Thus if a theory does have non-trivial infinitesimal gauge symmetries, one can only hope the pairing is non-degenerate on the smooth set
quotient
113
113

113

This assumes all infinitesimal gauge symmetries are integrable. The tangent bundle on the quotient smooth set
corresponds to the quotient of
T\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
by the induced pushforward action of the gauge symmetries.

\mathcal{F}/\mathrm{Diff}^{\mathcal{L},\mathrm{gauge}}(\mathcal{F})
from (
170
). The pair of the quotient of on-shell fields by gauge
symmetries with the induced presymplectic two-form

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}/\mathrm{Diff}^{\mathcal{L},\mathrm{gauge}}(\mathcal{F})\,,\,\bar{\Omega}_{\mathcal{L}}\big)

(201)

is known as the
reduced covariant phase space
. We highlight that the presymplectic
2
-form does descend to the
T\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}/\mathrm{Diff}^{\mathcal{L},\mathrm{gauge}}(\mathcal{F})

quotient since finite local symmetries preserve it, by Lem.
7.21
.

In the case where
N\equiv N\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow N\times\mathbbm{R}
is a Cauchy surface, and so in particular no gauge symmetries
exist
114
114

114

More generally, Cauchy surfaces may be formulated for the quotient of field configurations by gauge symmetries instead.
Hence, with initial data uniquely determining an on-shell field, up a to gauge transformation.
We will not expand on the details here.
, then one can transport the presymplectic structure along the defining isomorphism of
Def.
6.30
to the smooth set of initial data on the Cauchy surface.

Definition 7.42

(
Non-covariant phase space associated to Cauchy surface).

The
non-covariant phase space
of
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})

with respect to the Cauchy surface
N=N\times\{0\}\hookrightarrow N\times\mathbbm{R}
is defined as the presymplectic smooth set

\big(\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(N)\,,\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{Cau}}\big)\,,

where
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{Cau}}
is the pullback of
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}
along the isomorphism

\mathrm{Cau}_{N}=(-)|_{N}\circ j^{\infty}:\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(N)
.

Of course, for this to make sense, the correct notion of a tangent bundle on
\mathrm{InData}_{\mathcal{L}}(N)\hookrightarrow\mathbold{\Gamma}_{N}(J^{\infty}_{M}F|_{N})

is assumed. We will not go
into the technical details here,
115
115

115

Nevertheless, these follow immediately abstractly in the infinitesimally thickened setting of
[
GS24
]
.

but the interested reader may verify that this is the subspace of

\mathbold{\Gamma}_{N}\big(VJ^{\infty}_{M}F\big)\,,

consisting of (plots of) sections which factor through the (synthetic) tangent bundle of the prolongated shell
116
116

116

With the current technology,
this may be defined analogously to Def.
7.13
, i.e., as the (smooth) zero-locus of the pushforward

\mathsf{pr}EL_{*}:TJ^{\infty}_{M}F\rightarrow TJ^{\infty}_{M}F(V^{*}F\otimes\wedge^{d}T^{*}M)
.

S^{\infty}_{L}\rightarrow J^{\infty}_{M}F
.
A particularly simple but illustrative example is that of the free particle.

Example 7.43

(
Covariant and non-covariant free particle phase space).

Recall the free particle from Ex.
6.32
, with Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}=\partial_{t}\gamma^{a}\cdot\partial_{t}\gamma_{a}\cdot\mathrm{d}t
and Euler–Lagrange form

\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}(\gamma)=\partial_{t}^{2}\gamma\cdot\mathrm{d}t
. Reducing the presymplectic current of the
O(n)
-model from Ex.
7.11

to the particle case, or by calculating directly,

\omega_{\mathcal{L}}=\delta\gamma^{a}\wedge\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{a})\quad\in\;\;\Omega_{\mathrm{loc}}^{0,2}\big(\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\big)\,.

Transgression along the Cauchy surface
\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
reduces to evaluation at
t=0
, so that

\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\{0\}}=\delta\gamma^{a}\wedge\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{a})\big|_{t=0}\quad\in\;\;\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)

which further restricts to a presymplectic 2-form on the on-shell field space of
\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
.
To be more explicit, recall that by linearity the tangent bundle of paths is
T\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\cong\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\times\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
(Rem.
2.16
). Hence the tangent vectors over a path
\gamma\in\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
are

b^{a}\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma^{a}}\big|_{\gamma}\equiv(\gamma,b)\quad\in\quad T_{\gamma}\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\cong\{\gamma\}\times\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\,,

that is, a copy of all paths. These are represented by
\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{s}
-plots of the form
\gamma+s\cdot b
.
It follows that the presymplectic
2
-form
\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\{0\}}
is given by the smooth map

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\{0\}}:T\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\times_{\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})}T\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}

\displaystyle(\gamma,b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle\longmapsto b^{a}_{1}(0)\cdot\partial_{t}b_{2a}(0)-b_{2}^{a}(0)\cdot\partial_{t}b_{1a}(0)\,,

which is in particular constant along the base
\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
. From this form, it can be directly checked that the map is fiber-wise nondegenerate,
i.e., a genuine
symplectic
2-form. However, since
b_{1},b_{2}
are arbitrary paths in
\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, the value does depend on the chosen transgressing
submanifold, i.e., the chosen time instant
0\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
.

Let us now restrict this
2
-form to the on-shell field space
\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
. By representing tangent vectors
at
\gamma
via
\gamma+s\cdot b
, or otherwise, it follows that the pushforward of the Euler–Lagrange operator and the corresponding Jacobi equation
(
188
) is

\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}_{*}(\gamma,b)=(\partial_{t}^{2}\gamma,\partial_{t}^{2}b)=0
. Thus tangent vectors to lines are also lines, and so the tangent bundle
(Def.
7.13
) to the on-shell fields
\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
is given by the smooth subset

\displaystyle T\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\cong\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\times\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\cong\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\times\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\,.

The on-shell restriction of the
2
-form
\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\{0\}}
is given by exactly the same formula, with domain lines rather than all paths,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}:=\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\{0\}}|_{\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})}:T\mathrm{Lines}\times_{\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})}T\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}

\displaystyle(\gamma,b_{1},b_{2})

\displaystyle\longmapsto b^{a}_{1}(0)\cdot\partial_{t}b_{2\,a}(0)-b_{2}^{a}(0)\cdot\partial_{t}b_{1\,a}(0)\,,

In contrast to the off-shell symplectic
2
-form, this is in fact independent of the transgressing time instant, as guaranteed by
Lem.
7.37
, since both tangent vectors appearing are
lines
, i.e. necessarily of the form

b_{1,2}=w_{1,2}\cdot t+d_{1,2}\,\in\,C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t},\mathbbm{R}^{d})
. This completes the explicit description of the covariant
phase space (Def.
7.40
) of the free particle

\big(\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d}),\,\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}\big)\,.

Moving onto the non-covariant phase space associated to the Cauchy surface
\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
, recall the initial data “Cauchy diffeomorphism” from Eq. (
169
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cau}_{\{0\}}:\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\;\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\;T\mathbbm{R}^{d}\cong\mathbbm{R}^{d}\times\mathbbm{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\gamma=v\cdot t+c

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;(c,v)\,,

in which case the notion of a tangent bundle on the initial data smooth set, and also the pushforward along the isomorphism, is transparent.
Explicitly, let
\{x^{a},p^{a}\}
be the canonical coordinates on
T\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, then the pushforward acts on tangent vectors by

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Cau}_{\{0\}})_{*\gamma}:T_{\gamma}\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\cong\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\;\xlongrightarrow{\sim}\;T_{\mathrm{Cau}_{\{0\}}(\gamma)}(T\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle b=w\cdot t+d

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;b^{a}(0)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{a}}+\partial_{t}b^{a}(0)\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{a}}\,,

which for
b=w\cdot t+d
is simply
d^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{a}}+w^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{a}}
.
Thus, pulling back the covariant symplectic form
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}
by the inverse of
(\mathrm{Cau}_{\{0\}})_{*}
, we get the induced
symplectic form
\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{Cau}}
on
T\mathbbm{R}^{d}
given by

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{Cau}}:T(T\mathbbm{R}^{d})\times_{T\mathbbm{R}^{d}}T(T\mathbbm{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\;\xlongrightarrow{}\;\mathbbm{R}

\displaystyle\bigg(d_{1}^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{a}}+w_{1}^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{a}},\,d_{2}^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{a}}+w_{2}^{a}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial p^{a}}\bigg)

\displaystyle\;\longmapsto\;\;d_{1}^{a}\cdot w_{2\,a}-d_{2}^{a}\cdot w_{1\,a}\,.

Thus, in terms of coordinates, the non-covariant phase space of the free particle, associated with the time instant

\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
, is the symplectic manifold

\big(T\mathbbm{R}^{d},\,dx^{a}\wedge\mathrm{d}p_{a}\big)\,,

which, under the isomorphism
T\mathbbm{R}^{d}\xrightarrow{\sim}T^{*}\mathbbm{R}^{d}
induced by the Euclidean metric, is precisely
the canonical cotangent bundle symplectic manifold.

Covariant Poisson brackets

We conclude this first part of the series by detailing the transgressed version of the Hamiltonian current condition, and hence defining an (off-shell)
Poisson bracket on the algebra of Hamiltonian functionals on
\mathcal{F}
corresponding to the (chosen) transgressed symplectic
2
-form
\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}
.
For spacetimes of the form
N\times\mathbbm{R}
, and modulo Rem.
7.15
, this same story applies to the case of on-shell Hamiltonian
functionals and hence defining a canonical Poisson bracket on the covariant phase space
(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}},\Omega_{\mathcal{L}})
, corresponding to the
canonical on-shell transgressed presymplectic
2
-form.

Definition 7.44

(
Hamiltonian functionals).

A transgressed local functional

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\Sigma^{d-1}}=\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}})\equiv\int_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})

is
Hamiltonian
for the presymplectic smooth set
(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})
, corresponding to a compact oriented submanifold

\Sigma^{d-1}\hookrightarrow M
, if there exists a local vector field
\mathcal{Z}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
such that

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}+\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\Sigma^{d-1}}=0\,.

The minimal subalgebra generated by transgressed Hamiltonian functionals over
\Sigma^{d-1}
is the
algebra of
Hamiltonian functionals
over
\Sigma^{d-1}

\mathrm{HamAlg}(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})\lhook\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\,.

Strictly speaking, we should be speaking of transgressed
Hamiltonian (functional) pairs

(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}},\mathcal{Z})\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\times\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})
,
for the same reasons as in the case of Hamiltonian vector fields and corresponding currents. We will suppress this point in favor of easing the heavy notation.

Remark 7.45

(
Hamiltonian currents vs. Hamiltonian functionals).

(i)
Assuming
\partial\Sigma^{d-1}=\emptyset
, then all Hamiltonian currents
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}\in\Omega^{d-1,0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}\times M)

transgress along
\Sigma^{d-1}
to yield examples of Hamiltonian functionals, since the Hamiltonian current condition (
195
)
transgresses to the Hamiltonian functional condition.

(ii)
If
\Sigma^{d-1}
has a boundary, then only the subset of strict Hamiltonian currents transgresses to Hamiltonian functionals
along
\Sigma^{d-1}
. However, these do not exhaust all transgressed Hamiltonian functionals. In particular, for the case of no boundary, the
Hamiltonian functional condition of Def.
7.44
demands the corresponding current condition
117
117

117

This statement is
the converse to Stoke’s Theorem (see
[
La15
, §7.3]
).
The exact form on the right-hand side is possibly non-local in the field space.
, i.e.,

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}+\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}|_{\Sigma^{d-1}}=\mathrm{d}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(...)
being exact, only when restricted to the
submanifold
\Sigma^{d-1}
.

(iii)
Crucially, however, this does not imply the Hamiltonian current condition over
M
, i.e.,

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}+\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}}
being
\mathrm{d}_{M}
-exact on the full spacetime
M
.
An explicit such case is considered in Ex.
7.51
.

The algebra of Hamiltonian functionals over
\Sigma^{d-1}
is comprised of products of transgressed local functionals

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z},\Sigma^{d-1}}
over
\Sigma^{d-1}
, each of which satisfies the Hamiltonian condition for some local vector field
\mathcal{Z}
.
Of course, any Hamiltonian functional satisfies the Hamiltonian condition, however potentially via a smooth but
non-local

(functional) vector field.

Lemma 7.46

(
Hamiltonian functionals are Hamiltonian).

Any Hamiltonian functional
\bar{\mathcal{H}}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\in\mathrm{HamAlg}(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})
given by

\bar{\mathcal{H}}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{n},\Sigma^{d-1}}=\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}})\cdot\ldots\cdot\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{n}})\quad:\quad\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}

satisfies the Hamiltonian condition

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}+\delta\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}=0\,,

for the smooth vector field
\mathcal{Z}:=\sum_{i=0}^{n}\big(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdots\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\mathcal{Z}_{i},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdots\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{n},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big)\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{i}\,\in\,\mathcal{X}(\mathcal{F})
where
(\hat{-})
means omit.

Proof.

Recall that the algebra of Hamiltonian functionals is generated by transgressions of particular local
(d-1,0)
-form currents, and hence the
differential
\delta:\mathrm{HamAlg}(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})\rightarrow\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{F})
makes sense as the unique derivation extension
of that on charges
(Lem.
7.38
). We prove the statement for
n=2
, with the general case following by induction.
Consider two transgressed Hamiltonian functionals
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}},\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}
, and their product Hamiltonian functional

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F})\,.

By the derivation property of the vertical differential, it immediately follows that

\displaystyle\delta(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}})

\displaystyle=\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}+\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}

\displaystyle=-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}-\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}

\displaystyle=-\iota_{\big(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{1}+\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\cdot\mathcal{Z}_{2}\big)}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\,.

∎

Having identified the algebra of (smooth) Hamiltonian functionals of the presymplectic smooth set
(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})
,
the definition of the corresponding Poisson bracket follows exactly as in finite-dimensional (pre)symplectic geometry.

Definition 7.47

(
Off-shell Poisson bracket).

Let
\Sigma^{d-1}
be a compact oriented submanifold. The
Poisson bracket
of Hamiltonian functionals

\displaystyle\{-,-\}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}:\mathrm{HamAlg}(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})\times\mathrm{HamAlg}(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathrm{HamAlg}(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})

of the presymplectic smooth set
(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})
is defined by

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\,,\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big\}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}:=-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}

for any two transgressed Hamiltonian functionals, and extended as a bi-derivation to the full algebra of Hamiltonian functionals.

It is not hard to see on arbitrary Hamiltonian functionals, given by products of transgressed Hamiltonian functionals,
the Poisson bracket coincides with the contraction of the presymplectic 2-form with the corresponding non-local
vector fields from Lem.
7.46
.

It remains to show that the bracket is well-defined, i.e., indeed independent of the representative currents and vector fields,
and further mapping into Hamiltonian functionals, rather than arbitrary smooth functionals. Moreover, it remains to justify
the name
Poisson
, i.e., that it does actually satisfy the Jacobi identity.

Lemma 7.48

(
Poisson bracket is Lie).

The bracket of Def.
7.47
is well-defined and satisfies the Jacobi identity, turning the algebra of Hamiltonian
functionals into a Poisson algebra

\Big(\mathrm{HamAlg}\big(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big),\,\{-,-\}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\Big)\,.

Proof.

We show the statement for transgressed Hamiltonian functionals, with the general case following by the biderivation property.
To see that the bracket is independent of the chosen representative currents, recall by Lem.
7.38

that for any two local currents

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}},\,\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}}
such that their transgression/integral along
\Sigma^{d-1}
agrees

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}=\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}
,
it follows that their differentials also agree
\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}=\delta\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}
.
Assuming now the transgressed functionals are both Hamiltonian with respect to the vector fields
\mathcal{Z}_{2},\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}
,
respectively, it follows that

\displaystyle\big\{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\,,\,\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big\}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}:

\displaystyle=-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\hat{\mathcal{Z}}_{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}=\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\delta\hat{\mathcal{H}}_{\hat{\mathcal{Z}_{2}},\Sigma^{d-1}}

\displaystyle=\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}=-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}

\displaystyle=\big\{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\,,\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big\}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\,.

The fact that the image of the bracket sits inside Hamiltonian functionals follows by applying the local Cartan calculus
under the integral, giving

\delta\,\big\{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}}\,,\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big\}_{\Sigma^{d-1}}=\delta\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}=\iota_{[\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{Z}_{2}]}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}\,.

Finally, the Jacobi identity follows from the same calculation as that of Hamiltonian currents (
7.2
)
(where the Jacobi identity however fails). That is, for any three transgressed Hamiltonian functionals

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}},\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}},\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{3},\Sigma^{d-1}}
, we have the trivial vanishing

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{3}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}
by degree reasons. Applying the (transgressed)
vertical differential (Lem.
7.38
) on both sides and computing using the local Cartan
calculus under the integral yields

0=\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{[\mathcal{Z}_{2},\mathcal{Z}_{3}]}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}+\mathrm{cyc}(1,2,3)\,,

which yields exactly the Jacobi identity for the Poisson bracket via Def.
7.47
.
∎

Even though (transgressed) Hamiltonian functionals are not exhausted by transgressions of Hamiltonian currents
(Rem.
7.45
), the local ‘Poisson’ Lie algebras of Hamiltonian pairs of
Lem.
7.48
and (
198
) do map into
the Poisson Lie algebra of Hamiltonian functionals upon transgression, providing plenty of particular instances
of Hamiltonian functionals and their Poisson brackets.

Corollary 7.49

(
Brackets of Hamiltonian currents and functionals).

Let
\Sigma^{d-1}\hookrightarrow M
be a compact oriented submanifold.

(i)
If
\Sigma^{d-1}
is without boundary, then transgression
\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}:\mathrm{HamPrs}(\mathcal{F}\times M,\omega_{\mathcal{L}})\rightarrow\mathrm{HamAlg}(\mathcal{F},\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}})
intertwines the bracket of Hamiltonian pairs (Def.
7.29
)
and the corresponding Poisson bracket

\displaystyle\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big(\{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}},\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\}_{H}\big)=\big\{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}},\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big\}\quad:\quad\mathcal{F}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow\mathbbm{R}\;.

In particular, the transgression over
\Sigma^{d-1}
maps the central
extension local Poisson Lie algebra of Lem.
7.31
into the Poisson Lie algebra of Hamiltonian functionals
over
\Sigma^{d-1}
of Lem
7.48
.

(ii)
If
\Sigma^{d-1}
is with boundary, then the same holds for the subset transgressed strict Hamiltonian currents. In particular,
the transgression over
\Sigma^{d-1}
maps the Lie algebra of strict Hamiltonian Pairs of (
198
)
into the Poisson Lie algebra of Hamiltonian functionals over
\Sigma^{d-1}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately since the Poisson bracket of Hamiltonian functionals is defined via
\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}
which is the transgression
of
\omega_{\mathcal{L}}
, while the bracket of Hamiltonian currents is defined via the latter presymplectic current. Explicitly, for a submanifold without boundary,
the Hamiltonian current condition (Eq. (
195
)) transgresses to the Hamiltonian functional condition
(Def.
7.44
) since the
\mathrm{d}_{M}-
exact terms vanish upon integration. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\tau_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\big(\{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}},\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\}_{H}\big)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}=-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\int_{\Sigma^{d-1}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}

\displaystyle=-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{1}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{2}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L},\Sigma^{d-1}}=\big\{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{1},\Sigma^{d-1}},\,\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z}_{2},\Sigma^{d-1}}\big\}

The case of transgression over manifolds with boundary follows identically, since no
\mathrm{d}_{M}
-exact terms appear in the strict Hamiltonian current condition.
∎

Remark 7.50

(
Poisson algebra of the covariant phase space).

Consider the case of a product space-time
M=N\times\mathbbm{R}
, whereby there exists a canonical on-shell presymplectic 2-form making up
the covariant phase space (Def.
7.40
)

(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}},\Omega_{\mathcal{L}})\,.

Assuming the requirements of Rem.
7.15
are met, so that the local Cartan calculus descends to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\times M

and hence further transgresses to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}
as in Lem.
7.38
, then the above discussion follows verbatim for the
case of on-shell Hamiltonian functionals of
(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}},\Omega_{\mathcal{L}})
, and the canonical Poisson algebra of functionals they generate, determined
by the canonical presymplectic
2
-form. In more detail, these are generated by on-shell transgressed functionals

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z},N}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}})
that satisfy the on-shell Hamiltonian condition

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}+\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z},N}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}=0
for some on-shell vector field

\mathcal{Z}|_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}})
. Modulo Rem.
7.15
, these are represented by off-shell functionals

\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z},N}\in C^{\infty}(\mathcal{F})
that satisfy the off-shell Hamiltonian functional condition, up to the transgression a differential operator
applied to the Euler–Lagrange form

\iota_{\mathcal{Z}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}+\delta\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{Z},N}=\int_{N}\mathcal{J}^{I}\partial_{I}\mathcal{E}\mathcal{L}\,.

Example 7.51

(
Free particle covariant Poisson algebra).

Along the lines of Ex.
7.43
,
recall the free particle description of the off-shell presymplectic current

\omega_{\mathcal{L}}=\delta\gamma^{a}\wedge\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{a})\quad\in\;\;\Omega_{\mathrm{loc}}^{0,2}\big(\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\big)\,.

and induced on-shell symplectic
2
-form

\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}=\delta\gamma^{a}\wedge\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{a})\big|_{t=t_{0}}\quad\in\;\;\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)\,,

which is independent of the chosen
\{t_{0}\}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
. Using the identification of the on-shell field space with the symplectic manifold

(T\mathbbm{R}^{d},\,\mathrm{d}x^{a}\wedge\mathrm{d}p_{a})
, via the diffeomorphism described in Ex.
7.43
, and the straightforward description of Hamiltonian
functions on the latter, it is often claimed that the on-shell functionals

\Gamma^{a}(t_{0}):=\gamma^{a}|_{t=t_{0}}\quad,\quad\dot{\Gamma}_{a}(t_{0}):=\partial_{t}\gamma_{a}|_{t=t_{0}}\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)

are both Hamiltonian (conjugate variables) for any
t_{0}\in\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
, with Hamiltonian vector fields

\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma^{a}(t_{0})}\overset{?}{=}\frac{\delta}{\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma^{a})}\quad,\quad\mathcal{Z}_{\dot{\Gamma}_{a}(t_{0})}:=-\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma^{a}}\quad\in\quad\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)\,,

respectively. The question mark on the former is intentional since this symbol does
not
define a local vector field on the field space.
Of course, this is the naive formula substitution of the canonical Hamiltonian vector fields corresponding to the non-covariant Hamiltonian
conjugate variables
x^{a},p^{a}\in C^{\infty}(T\mathbbm{R}^{d})
on the non-covariant phase space at
\{0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}
.
We now make precise what this is actually supposed to mean, thus justifying the its formal manipulation.

Firstly, we note the contraction of the (off-shell) symplectic current
\omega_{\mathcal{L}}
with the latter well-defined local vector field

-\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma^{a}}
gives

\displaystyle\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{\dot{\Gamma}_{a}(t_{0})}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}=\iota_{-\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma^{a}}}\big(\delta\gamma^{b}\wedge\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{b})\big)=-\delta^{a}_{\,b}\cdot\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{b})+\delta\gamma^{a}\cdot\partial_{t}(\delta^{a}_{\,b})=-\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{a})\,,

and so
\partial_{t}\gamma_{a}\in\Omega^{0,0}\big(\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\times\mathbbm{R}^{1}_{t}\big)
is an off-shell Hamiltonian current
(Def.
195
). This immediately transgresses to the off-shell Hamiltonian functional

\partial_{t}\gamma_{a}|_{t=t_{0}}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)
. Since
-\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma^{a}}
is tangent to the
on-shell fields
\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
, this indeed restricts to give the
Hamiltonian
functional

\dot{\Gamma}_{a}(t_{0}):=\partial_{t}\gamma_{a}|_{t=t_{0}}\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)

which corresponds to the observable that measures the velocity (momentum) of the particle moving on a line at
t=t_{0}
.

Next, consider the well-defined off-shell local vector field given by

t\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma_{a}}\in\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)\,

which is the vector field
\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\meno}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\meno}$}}}\mathrel{\mkern-3.0mu}\rightarrow T\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
given by
\gamma\rightarrow(\gamma,t\cdot e_{a})
.
In other words, it is the (constant) vector field on the space of paths that assigns (to any path
\gamma
) the tangent vector path

b=1\cdot t\cdot e_{a}+0
of constant speed
v=1
in the direction
e_{a}\in\mathbbm{R}^{d}
, passing through the origin. Since tangent vectors
to the on-shell fields
\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})
are also lines, it follows that this vector field does restrict to an on-shell vector field.
Its contraction with the off-shell presymplectic current gives

\displaystyle\iota_{t\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma_{a}}}\omega_{\mathcal{L}}=t\cdot\delta^{a}_{\,b}\cdot\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{b})+\delta\gamma_{a}\cdot\partial_{t}(t\cdot\delta^{a}_{\,b})=t\cdot\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{a})-\delta\gamma^{a}\,,

which, however, still does
not
exhibit
\delta\gamma^{a}
as a Hamiltonian current. Nevertheless, the
transgression along
t=0
does exhibit

\gamma^{a}|_{t=0}\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)

as an
off-shell
Hamiltonian functional for
\Omega_{\mathcal{L},0}\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathbold{P}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)
, which restricts to
an on-shell Hamiltonian functional for the canonical symplectic
2
-form, i.e.,

\displaystyle\iota_{t\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma_{a}}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}=\big(t\cdot\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{a})-\delta\gamma^{a}\big)|_{t=0}=-\delta\gamma^{a}|_{t=0}\,.

Consequently,

\Gamma^{a}(0)=\gamma^{a}|_{t=0}\quad\in\quad C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\big(\mathrm{Lines}(\mathbbm{R}^{d})\big)

is indeed an on-shell Hamiltonian functional for the on-shell vector field

\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma^{a}(0)}:=t\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma^{a}}\,.

Analogously

\Gamma^{a}(t_{0})=\gamma^{a}|_{t=t_{0}}

is Hamiltonian for any
t_{0}
, with corresponding vector field

\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma^{a}(t_{0})}:=(t-t_{0})\cdot\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma^{a}}\,.

Consequently, the induced Poisson bracket of the two Hamiltonian functionals is given by

\displaystyle\big\{\Gamma^{a}(t_{0})\,,\,\dot{\Gamma}_{b}(t_{0})\big\}:

\displaystyle=-\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma^{a}(t_{0})}}\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{\dot{\Gamma}_{a}(t_{0})}}\Omega_{\mathcal{L}}=+\iota_{\mathcal{Z}_{\Gamma^{a}(t_{0})}}\delta(\partial_{t}\gamma_{a})\big|_{t=t_{0}}

\displaystyle=\delta^{a}_{\,b}

as expected by the non-covariant Poisson algebra structure. Since any local functional with higher jet dependence than
1
vanishes
on the space of on-shell fields, it follows that the
Poisson algebra of Hamiltonian functionals is generated by these two (families) of transgressed functionals.
We leave it to the reader to verify that, under the Cauchy isomorphism of Ex.
7.43
, the local vector field

t\frac{\delta}{\delta\gamma^{a}}
maps to the canonical Hamiltonian vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial p_{a}}
on
T\mathbbm{R}^{d}
,
which exhibits
x^{a}
as a Hamiltonian function, conjugate to
p_{a}
. This shows that, even though the notation used is mathematically ill-defined,
the resulting statement of the position and velocity observables being Hamiltonian functionals and ‘conjugate’ is nevertheless correct.

A similar story can be repeated in the case of the
{\rm O}(n)
-model’s (e.g. scalar field) and electromagnetism’s conjugate variables
viewed as covariant phase space Hamiltonian functionals, and their corresponding Hamiltonian vector fields.

8
Outlook

In conclusion, we have shown that smooth sets constitute an excellent context for formulating classical field theory
in its vanilla form for plain bosonic fields. In follow-up articles in
this series
[
GS24
]
[
GSS24
]
, we will generalize smooth sets in various natural ways enhancing this correspondence to

(a)
infinitesimal structure / perturbative field theory,

(b)
fermionic field theory, and

(c)
higher gauge theory by invoking, in turn, synthetic differential supergeometry and higher topos theory.

We briefly outline some of the basic ideas (cf.

[
FSS14
]
[
AY23
]
[
Sc24
]
[
GSS24
]
):

•

Synthetic geometry and infinitesimal structure.

A remarkable (if maybe underappreciated) fact of differential geometry says that passage from smooth manifolds to their ordinary
real algebras of smooth functions is a full embedding into the opposite category
\mathrm{CAlg}_{{}_{\mathbbm{R}}}
of commutative real algebras
(“Milnor’s exercise”, cf.
[
KMS93
, §35.8-10]
):

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SmthMfd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CAlg}_{{}_{\mathbbm{R}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\longmapsto}

This means that smooth manifolds behave more like affine schemes in algebraic geometry than the usual definitions may suggest,
thus providing access to analogous constructions. In particular, we may regard any full subcategory of
\mathrm{CAlg}_{{}_{\mathbbm{R}}}

larger than the image of (
•
‣
8
) as
being
a category of “generalized manifolds” which
do not exist as point-set models but (only) through their would-be algebras of functions.

Concretely, since the algebra of smooth functions on the
r
-th order
infinitesimal neighborhood
of the origin in
\mathbbm{R}^{m}

should be the real polynomials in
m
variables modulo the relation that any
r+1
st power of them vanishes, we may take this to
be the dual definition of the
infinitesimal halo

\mathbbm{D}^{m}_{r}\hookrightarrow\mathbbm{R}^{m}
around the origin and take the category
of
infinitesimally thickened
Cartesian spaces to be the full subcategory on the objects of the following form:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CAlg}_{{}_{\mathbbm{R}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}^{m}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes_{{}_{\mathbbm{R}}}\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon_{1},...\epsilon_{m}]/(\epsilon^{r+1})\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\longmapsto}

This category becomes a site by declaring the open covers to be of the form
\big\{U_{i}\times\mathbbm{D}^{m}_{r}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\iota_{i}\times\mathrm{id}}}\mathbbm{R}^{k}\times\mathbbm{D}^{m}_{r}\big\}_{i\in I}
for the
\iota_{i}
constituing a (differentiably) good open cover of ordinary Cartesian spaces as before. The resulting sheaf
topos of
formal smooth sets

\mathrm{Frml}\mathrm{SmthSet}\;:=\;\mathrm{Sh}(\mathrm{Th}\mathrm{CartSp})

is known as the
Cahiers topos
, a “well-adapted model” for “synthetic differential geometry”. Here smooth manifolds (and generally smooth sets)
co-exist with actual infinitesimal objects such as the first-order infinitesimal interval
\mathbbm{D}^{1}_{1}
. Note that the latter contains just a single point

\ast\simeq\mathbbm{R}^{0}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\hskip 8.19447pt\iota\hskip 8.19447pt}_{\exists!}}\mathbbm{D}^{1}_{1}

and yet is larger than that point (received more plots, namely from other infinitesimal).

For instance, the tangent bundle
TM\xrightarrow{p}M
of a smooth manifold
M
(itself a smooth manifold) appears now literally as the space
of images of the infinitesimal interval in
M
, in that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\mathbbm{D}^{1}_{1},M]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[\ast,M]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\mathrlap{\,.}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces[\iota,\mathbbm{D}^{1}_{1}]\;\;}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proceeding along these lines, one finds a convenient and powerful realization of plenty of field-theoretic concepts in
\mathrm{Frml}\mathrm{SmthSet}
(cf.
[
KS17
]
).
In particular,
every
tangent bundle appearing in this first part of the series is in fact recovered as an example of the synthetic
tangent bundle construction, with the target being instead the field space
\mathcal{F}
, a subspace thereof, or the infinite jet bundle
J^{\infty}_{M}F

(viewed as formal smooth sets). Similarly, the set of infinite jets of the field bundle
F\rightarrow M
arises as sections over infinitesimal
neighborhoods
\mathbbm{D}_{p}\hookrightarrow M
of the base and, in fact,
the infinite jet bundle as a (formal) smooth set arises via a natural categorical construction. Moreover, the perturbation theory of a local field theory
(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{L})
around a point
\phi_{0}\in\mathcal{F}
in field space may be rigorously defined and described as the
actual
restriction of the Lagragrian to the infinitesimal neighborhood
\mathbbm{D}_{\phi_{0}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}
of the chosen field.

•

Supergeometry and fermionic fields.

In this vein, also fermionic supergeometry finds its natural home simply by further enlarging the ambient category of commutative algebras (
•
‣
8
) to that of
\mathbb{Z}/2
-graded commutative algebras, to be denoted
\mathrm{sCAlg}_{{}_{\mathbbm{R}}}
.
Here we want the “smooth functions” on a purely odd super-space
\mathbbm{R}^{0|q}
to be the Grassmann-algebra
\wedge^{\bullet}\big((\mathbbm{R}^{q})^{\ast}\big)

on
q
generators, and hence we may define
super-Cartesian spaces
to be those which constitute the following full subcategory:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{SupCartSp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{sCAlg}_{{}_{\mathbbm{R}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{R}^{k|q}\times\mathbbm{D}^{m}_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k})\otimes\wedge^{\bullet}\big((\mathbbm{R}^{q})^{\ast}\big)\otimes\mathbbm{R}[\epsilon_{1},...\epsilon_{m}]/(\epsilon^{r+1})\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{\infty}(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\longmapsto}

With the open covers of such super-Cartesian spaces defined in the same
manner as before, we obtain the topos of
super smooth sets

\mathrm{Sup}\mathrm{SmthSet}\;:=\;\mathrm{Sh}\big(\mathrm{SupCartSp}\big)\,.

This topos is the home of classical fermionic fields (cf.
[
KS98
]
[
Sa08
]
). In particular, it is the internal hom,
mapping space construction that encodes fermionic field spaces as used in the physics literature. Such spaces often have
no underlying points
at all, and the non-trivial information is instead encoded via higher
\mathbbm{R}^{0|q}
-plots.

•

Higher geometry and gauge fields.

On the other hand, the characteristic property of (gauge) bosons is that their field spaces have a relaxed notion of
equality
,
in that two (plots/families of) gauge fields

\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\,:\,\mathbbm{R}^{k}\xrightarrow{\phantom{---}}\mathrm{Fields}

may be nominally distinct and yet identified via gauge transformations
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}\!\!\!}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
,
that are invertible and may be associatively composed:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{R}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fields}\mathrlap{\,.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\circ g}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

This means that the plots of gauge field spaces no longer form plain sets, but
groupoids
(exposition of groupoids includes
[
We96
]
).

Furthermore, for
higher
gauge fields, two such gauge transformations, in turn, may be nominally distinct and yet identified by “gauge-of-gauge transformations”

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbbm{R}^{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fields}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.3906,0.0195}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Rightarrow}
{\color[rgb]{0.7852,0.3906,0.0195}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

satisfying a 2-dimensional analog of composition and associativity, as schematically indicated here:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{\scalebox{.7}{
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}
$(\Delta^{0})$
}}\Phi}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{\scalebox{.7}{
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}
$(\Delta^{1})$
}}\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{\scalebox{.9}{
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}
$\scriptstyle(\Delta^{2})$
\;\;\;\,
}}g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h\circ g}

\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.0781,0.5508,0.0391}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu(g,h)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrlap{\,,}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\prime\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathllap{\scalebox{.9}{
\color[rgb]{0.5,0.5,0.5}
$\scriptstyle(\Delta^{3})$
}}}

and so on to ever higher order gauge transformations, now making the plots form
higher groupoids
, which may be thought of as Kan simplicial sets, for exposition see
[
Fr12
, §7]
).

Hence the plots of a higher gauge field spaces form generally not a plain set but a Kan simplicial set, organizing generally not into a plain presheaf but into a (Kan-)
simplicial presheaf
(cf.
[
Ja87
]
).

\mathrm{PSh}\big(\mathrm{SupCartSp},\,\mathrm{sSet}_{\mathrm{Kan}}\big)\;=\;\Bigg\{\!\!\!\hbox to186.09pt{\vbox to34.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 93.0417pt\lower-17.20334pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-93.0417pt}{-11.04361pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

g_{i}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbbm{R}^{k},G)\\
A_{i}=g_{i}A_{i-1}g_{i}^{-1}+g_{i}\mathrm{d}g_{i}^{-1}\end{array}\!\!\!\right\}.\end{array}

(202)

Here a plot of
\mathbf{B}G_{\mathrm{conn}}
is a
G
-gauge field configuration on the probe chart
\mathbbm{R}^{n}
, given by a
\mathfrak{g}=\mathrm{Lie}(G)
-valued
1-form
A
(a gauge potential) and an equivalence of plots is a smooth
G
-valued function
g
on the probe chart relating two such 1-forms by the usual
gauge transformations (
171
). As hinted in Rem.
6.37
, the
set
of
G
-gauge fields on a
spacetime manifold
M
is then given by maps
M\rightarrow\mathbf{B}G_{\mathrm{conn}}
, with the internal hom object
\big[M,\mathbf{B}G_{\mathrm{conn}}\big]

encoding the gauge equivalency transformations between any two gauge fields via its
\Delta^{1}
-plots.

As before, this means that the naive formulas known from physics apply
on probes
and the (now: higher) topos theory takes care of producing
global structures from these probes. We will further explain how this works in
[
GSS24
]
.

Generally, we emphasize that even without considering gauge fields and/or gauge symmetries, higher structures are implicit throughout the treatment
of a local field theory. In particular, the finite (and infinitesimal) symmetries of a Lagrangian field theory (Def.
3.23
)
can be interpreted as saying that the
Lagrangian is preserved
up to homotopy
, in a precise sense. Similarly, a careful discussion of the corresponding conserved and Hamiltonian
currents shows that higher structures in the guise of infinitesimal versions of
\infty
-groupoids, i.e.,
L_{\infty}
-algebras, are secretly
dwelling within local classical field theories (Rem.
7.33
).
